Game Mastery
 Your Role as Game Master 
Before we rush into the rules the game: the details of combat; the 
measurement of time and how you should record encumbrance, it is 
always worth restating the role of the Game Master. If you are 
reading this, we assume it is because you have decided to take on 
the mantle of the Game Master. You will play the monsters, the allies, 
the world and the plot – and this section has been written from the 
ground up to provide you with the tools necessary to facilitate hours 
of satisfactory adventuring for you and your friends. 
 
Tests 
The core mechanic of JEM is the attribute and skill test. A test is 
made by a character when the game master calls for it. To make a 
test, the character rolls 1d20 and compares the number to their listed 
Target Number for that level. If the result is equal to or higher than 
the Target Number, then they have succeeded.  
 
For example, Gundarr the Dwarf’s player wishes to break down a 
locked door using the Force Doors skill. Gundarr is of 4th level and 
his Experience Path is the Jack. He has Journeyman proficiency, 
meaning he needs a 12 or more to successfully break down the door. 
 
Difficulty Levels  
Often a simple skill check against a character’s skill will suffice, 
however, there are times when the GM may wish to alter the chances 
of a skill being successfully used. Using the example above, it may 
be that the door Gundarr is trying to break down is made of rotten 
wood and is especially weak or is particularly strong and bound in 
stout iron bands. In such cases, the game master may wish to adjust 
the difficulty of the check, in order to make it easier or harder to 
succeed. A simple, unmodified skill check is referred to as 
Challenging but there are other difficulty levels, listed below, which 
provide modifiers to the Test roll: 
 
DIFFICULTY LEVELS 
Difficulty 
Modifier to Roll 
Routine 
+9 
Very Easy 
+6 
Easy 
+3 
Challenging 
- 
Hard 
-3 
Very Hard 
-6 
Formidable 
-9 
 
Opposed Tests 
Sometimes a creature will end up in an opposed test with another 
creature, such as a warrior trying to push an ogre off a cliff using 
Strength or a wizard in a mental dual with a foe using Intellect.  
 
When completing Opposed tests, only one participant rolls the test, 
usually the creature that has used an action to initiate the opposed 
test (referred to on the table below as the ‘attacker’). To work out 
what the target number should be, consult the table below. 
 
Dramatic Tests 
Sometimes it will be necessary to have a series of multiple tests as 
part of a larger challenge. This is known as a Dramatic test. A 
Dramatic test consists of a number of rounds in which player 
characters can contribute to the overall success or failure of a 
particular moment. A Dramatic test works as follows: 
 
The game master explains the possible outcome and then states how 
many successful skill and attribute tests must be made to succeed, 
as well as how many failures the Dramatic test can sustain until it is 
considered failed overall.  
 
Each round a character may make one skill or attribute test in 
contribution to the overall Dramatic test outcome. The game master 
should be willing to suggest the relevant skills and attributes and 
how they may be useful to succeeding overall in a Dramatic test. 
Additionally, the game master should also be willing to take 
suggestions from players as to what other skills and attributes may 
be relevant.  
 
What is important is that once a skill is used successfully once in a 
dramatic test, then it cannot be used again. During a Dramatic test, 
each skill and attribute may only be successfully used once. 
 
An example Dramatic test is provided below to aid clarity. 
 
Example Dramatic Test: Lost in the Blizzard 
The characters have gotten lost in an arctic region. Instead of a 
simple survival test to find shelter, the game master decides to make 
this a Dramatic test as a blizzard rolls in.  
 
The game master states that to find shelter and safety from the 
blizzard the party will need to make 4 successful skill tests before 3 
failures. 
 
They explain that the relevant skills and attributes for this test are: 
▶ END: A character may test END in order to help their comrades 
grit their teeth and push on through the blizzard. 
▶ Forage/Hunting: A character may test Forage/Hunting to find 
appropriately dry firewood.  
▶ Knowledge (Nature): A character may test Knowledge (Nature) 
in order to pass on information about this region, such as where 
natural shelter like caves may be. 
▶ Pray (-6): A character may test Pray as very hard (-6) to ask their 
gods to abate the terrible weather. 
▶ Survival (+3): A character may test Survival as easy (+3) to scout 
an appropriate place to camp. 
▶ Tracking: A character may test Tracking to follow a game trail 
perhaps to the shelter that is used by a large mammal like a cave 
bear or maybe to a trapper’s lodge. 
 
Players may of course suggest additional skills that a game master 
should consider. With each failure, the game master should 
introduce some hidden threat. In the above example it may provoke 
saves vs END, with characters suffering cold damage on a failure. If 
an overall success is achieved, then the Dramatic test should yield 
experience point rewards. An overall failure should result in 
significant setbacks and negative consequences. 
 
Dramatic tests can be used for any scenario that is complex and hard 
to manage mechanically. Examples may include: scaling a mountain, 
negotiating with a noble family, fighting for control of a sailing ship 
in a storm, escaping a burning city or any other like scenario. 
 
Critical Successes and Failures 
When a character makes a test that is a natural 20 on the d20 roll, 
then they have scored a ‘critical success’. Generally critical results 
are considered to have passed regardless of any other modifiers. 
Conversely, if a natural 1 is rolled then the test is considered a 
‘critical failure’ and the test is failed regardless of any other 
modifiers. 
 
FP Burn 
Any character may choose to ‘burn’ FP. This gives them a +1 bonus 
to any rolled test at the cost of 1FP. A creature may burn FP on a 
test after the dice has been rolled, turning a failure into success. FP 
may never be spent to increase a critical failure. Similarly, spending 
FP to bring a test to a 20 does not count as a critical success.

Game Mastery 
102 
 
Time 
Time in game is particularly important. Several units of time are used 
to measure the progress the party and the use of certain abilities in 
game. JEM commonly uses the following units of time: 
▶ Instantaneous: If a spell or other action is listed as happening 
instantaneously, it means it happens immediately, without delay. 
▶ Round: A round represents 1 minute. It is a common measure of 
action-usage in combat. 
▶ Turn: A turn represents 10 minutes of in game time. It is assumed 
that most exploration within the game takes place in turns, and that 
most combats are completed within 1 turn. 
 
Resting 
To restore HP and FP, a character generally must be able to rest, as 
detailed below: 
 
Extended Rests 
An extended rest is usually an 8-hour period. During this time, it is 
assumed that a character has time to eat, drink and sleep comfortably 
(perhaps in a bedroll, tent or a starship’s stateroom). After an 
extended rest, a character receives 1 hit point provided they had 
access to rations. It is assumed that characters will generally be 
asleep for roughly 6 hours of the 8. A character may only take one 
Extended Rest per day. 
 
Short Rests 
A short rest is a bit of breathing space. It might be time to check 
armour dents, change swords, clean gun barrels and grab something 
to eat. A short rest takes 1 hour, after which a character regains all 
of their lost Fatigue Points. A character may only gain the benefits 
of one short rest per day. 
 
Encumbrance 
Encumbrance is a measurement of what characters can carry. 
Encumbrance is abstracted into slots. Almost all equipment and 
carried items take one slot. Some items, however, may be stacked 
into multiples of a single slot. For example, 100 coins may occupy a 
single carry slot. A general rule, items never take more than one slot.  
 
Optional Rule: Encumbered or Unencumbered 
For most campaigns, it may not be within the game master’s 
interest to have how much a character is carrying affect their other 
abilities. However, in some campaigns such as those that focus on 
survivalism or progression tied closely to items, it may be worth 
considering how much a character is carrying and how that affects 
their overall functionality. If that is the case, it is recommended to 
use the following rules: 
▶ Unencumbered: If a character is using half or fewer of their total 
carrying capacity slots, then they are considered unencumbered and 
behave normally. Backpack  and other container slots are ignored 
when calculating this. 
▶ Lightly Encumbered: If a character is using more than half of 
their total carrying capacity slots but not all of them, then they are 
lightly encumbered. A lightly encumbered character reduces their 
movement by 33% (rounded down) but otherwise acts normally. 
▶ Heavily Encumbered: If a character is using more than all of their 
total carrying capacity slots then they are heavily encumbered. A 
heavily encumbered character reduces their movement by 66% 
(rounded down) and suffers a -3 penalty to all tests that require 
movement, and all attack tests. 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
Combat and Danger 
Combat is the cut and thrust of battle and proceeds as detailed here. 
Combat is split into a series of rounds (1-minute blocks of time) 
During a round of combat, each character can make one Move, and 
one Action. 
 
Actions 
Actions are a number of things a character can do in combat, such 
as attacking with a sword, casting a spell, shooting a crossbow or 
closing a portal to R’lyeh.  
 
The Combat Round 
A combat round is broken into the following steps each round: 
 
Combat Steps Each Round 
1. Declare Actions Step 
2. Initiative Step 
3. Move Step 
4. Missile Attacks Step 
5. Supernatural Abilities Step 
6. Melee Attacks Step 
7. Other Actions Step 
 
1. Declare Actions Step 
During this step all the players declare what their characters intend 
to do that round, such as ‘I will move forward and slash the goblin 
with my sword’ or ‘I will duck behind this wall and shoot at the 
Deep One’. While these descriptions can be fairly broad, the game 
master should insist on a few things: attacks must be declared as 
either Missile or Melee as well as the intended target; spells being 
cast must be specified (it is not enough to declare ‘I will cast a spell’ 
but the spell to be cast must be stated); all supernatural abilities must 
be declared. 
 
2. Initiative Step 
In this step, the players roll 1d6 and the game master rolls 1d6 for 
the enemies. The highest roll resolves their declared actions first 
(Steps 3 to 7), then the losing side does the same. A tie should be 
rerolled unless a character as a talent that affects this. 
 
3. Move Step 
Each creature may move up to its listed move value. 
 
4. Missile Attacks Step 
Weapons which use the ranged skill can now be used, such a bows, 
throwing daggers, and crossbows. 
 
5. Supernatural Abilities Step 
This is when characters use special actions that are supernatural. 
This might be a priest’s Banish Undead ability or a wizard casting 
Bolt. 
 
6. Melee Attacks Step 
Melee weapons may now be used, such as swords and laser blades. 
It is also when natural weapons such as claws are most commonly 
used. 
 
7. Other Actions Step 
This is when actions such as drinking potions, reloading a character’s 
revolver or trying to close the blast doors are resolved. A creature 
that took no other action may also move again, up to their listed 
move value. A creature that has not yet moved may do so now too. 
 
 


Game Mastery 
103 
 
Attacks and Damage 
Below are the rules for making attacks and damaging opponents in 
a game of JEM. 
 
Making an Attack 
If a character has declared they are making an attack, then in the 
appropriate Combat Step they are able to do so. Resolve using the 
following: 
 
1. Make a Challenging (+0) test with the appropriate skill for the 
weapon (which is listed in the weapon entry) This is called the 
attack test. For example, for attacks with a longsword, this is the 
Melee skill. 
 
2. If the attack test is a success, then the attack has scored a hit.  
 
3. The target makes an Armour Save, by rolling 1d6 for each 
damage received. If the result is a 6 or more, they have successfully 
deflected the incoming damage and ignore the strike. If they roll less 
than a 6 then they reduce their hit points by 1. Each armour has a 
specific number that it also saves on, such as leather armour needing 
a 5+. This means a 5 or a 6 will result in a success. Talents that affect 
saves do not affect armour saves, unless it is specifically mentioned. 
 
A natural roll of a 6 means that the strike has been deflected, 
regardless of any other modifiers. If a natural roll of 1 is made, then 
the damage is applied regardless of any other modifiers. 
 
Rend and Power Saves 
Many weapons and creature attacks will list a Rend value, such as 
Rend (1). This is a negative penalty applied to armour saves 
(including cover saves). For example, if a character has a 4+ armour 
save and is struck by an attack that has Rend (1) then they would 
need to roll a 5+ to succeed. Remember, a natural 6 on an armour 
save always succeeds, regardless of other modifiers (and a natural 1 
always fails). 
 
Sometimes a creature will have access to a power save. This is a 
special type of save that works exactly like armour saves and is 
rolled in addition to armour saves. Any failed armour saves may 
be rerolled if the creature has a power save. Nothing ever modifies 
a power save, under any circumstances and therefore power saves 
cannot be affected by something like the Rend weapon quality. 
Sometimes a power, spell, attack or other actions states that the 
damage caused ‘ignores all armour saves’ – this does not ignore a 
power save. 
 
Resistances and Vulnerabilities 
▶ Resistance: If a creature has resistance to a damage type, then 
any damage it takes from an attack is halved (rounded down), to a 
minimum of one. For example, if a creature with resistance to 
slashing damage is hit for 2 points of slashing from a longsword, 
then 1 point of damage is ignored automatically. The creature then 
rolls its armour saves against the remaining damage. 
▶ Vulnerability: If a creature has vulnerability to a damage type, 
then any damage it takes from an attack is doubled. For example, if 
a creature with vulnerability to slashing damage is hit for 2 points 
of slashing from a longsword, then it must make its armour saves 
against 4 points of damage. 
 
Damage and Death 
When a non-player character is reduced to 0 hit points they are 
killed. This is slightly different for player characters. When a player 
character is reduced to 0 hit points they are dying. The character is 
unconscious and must either be stabilised or healed to prevent 
their death. To stabilise a dying character, they must successfully be 
the subject of a Healing test. 
 
When a character is dying the player creates their death pool. This 
is a number of d6s equal to their proficiency level in END. For 
example, a character with Journeyman END would have a death 
pool of 2d6. At the end of each round, the player should roll their 
death pool of d6s and remove any natural 1s. Repeat this at the end 
of each round, removing the natural 1s each time. When the death 
pool is totally depleted and no dice remain, the character dies. If a 
character has 0 proficiency levels in END (Untrained) then they 
have a death pool consisting of a single d4. 
Dramatic Actions 
Some creatures have ‘dramatic actions’ listed in their statblock. A 
dramatic action does not cost a creature its action for that round – 
instead it is taken after any other step in the round (even after the 
enemy’s steps). Most dramatic actions are reserved for the most 
powerful of monsters. 
 
Saves 
Oftentimes a character is called upon to make a save. This is almost 
always to avoid the effects of something unpleasant, such as a spell 
or trap. This will be stated as a ‘save vs’ and then a stated attribute, 
such as a ‘save vs PER’. A save is simply a test taken for that core 
attribute.  
 
Critical Hits 
If the attack roll is a natural 20 then the hit scored is ‘critical’. The 
attacker should double their attack’s damage. The target has the 
option to soak the damage by destroying their own armour. If they 
do this, then they choose one piece of armour they are wearing to 
be destroyed but the damage from the attack is not doubled and they 
still receive their armour save.  
 
Cover 
Sometimes a creature is in cover, granting an extra save to reduce 
damage if an attack hits. This save is taken before any armour saves 
or power saves and is referred to as a cover save. A cover save is 
affected by anything that affects an armour save and similarly 
always succeeds on a natural 6 and fails on a natural 1. 
 
To benefit from a cover save, a creature must be at least 50% hidden 
from the would-be attacker. Similarly, in most circumstances a 
creature only gains the benefit of a cover save during the Missile 
Attacks Step. The best cover save a creature can hope for is a 4+.  
 
It is ultimately up to the game master as to what constitutes granting 
cover save, and some examples are given below: 
▶ 6+: hedges, bracken, etc. 
▶ 5+: ruins, debris, carriage doors, etc. 
▶ 4+: stone walls, castle battlements, etc. 
 
Surprise 
Sometimes one side in combat has the element of surprise over 
another. It is also possible that both sides blunder into each other 
without warning. When this is the case, the members of the side that 
might be surprised (which could be both) should save vs INT. On a 
failure, they are surprised and can make no actions during the first 
round of combat, including responsive actions such as dodging and 
parrying. 
 
Disengages and Attacks of Opportunity 
When a creature is adjacent (within 5’) of another creature in combat, 
they can be assumed to be ‘engaged’. This means that if an engaged 
creature leaves this 5’ adjacency, then the creature they are moving 
away from may make a free attack test with a melee weapon. This 
is called an attack of opportunity. A creature may declare it is using 
its action for a round to disengage, which means it may move away 
without provoking an attack of opportunity, but then it may not 
use its action to do anything else. 
 
Size of Combatants and Creatures 
There are six different categories of size: tiny, small, medium, large, 
huge and gargantuan.  
▶ Tiny creatures take up a negligible amount of space and up to 
four tiny creatures can fit in a 5’x5’ area.  
▶ Small creatures are about the size of a child and take up 5’x5’ 
space. 
▶ Medium creatures are about the size of an adult and take up 5’x5’ 
space.  
▶ Large creatures take up 10’x10’ space. Huge creatures take up 
15’x15’ space.  
▶ Gargantuan creatures take up to or more than a 20’x20’ space. 


Game Mastery 
104 
 
Combat Manoeuvres 
It is impossible for a game master to consider every single possible 
action a character may take in combat. A wide array of actions is not 
covered succinctly by the rules, such as pinning a foe to the wall 
with an arrow; disarming an opponent or jumping on a foe’s back. 
We call all these actions combat manoeuvres: actions that are made 
in combat against foes that do not cause weapon damage. If a 
character (either a player character or non-player character) wishes 
to perform a combat manoeuvre, use the following steps: 
 
1. The attacker makes a Challenging (+0) test with the appropriate 
skill for the weapon (which is listed in the weapon entry). This is 
the attack test. They must state what their intended action is, such 
as disarming their opponent, gouging their eye, tripping them up 
before rolling.  
 
2. The target now has a choice: they must decide before the attacker 
makes the attack test whether they accept the stated manoeuvre. If 
the target accepts the manoeuvre, the attacker makes the attack test 
and, if successful, then the target suffers the described manoeuvre. 
If the target does not accept the stated manoeuvre, then the attacker 
may still make a normal attack test for normal weapon damage 
instead of the stated effect.  
 
By allowing the target to decide whether to take damage from the 
attack or a described combat manoeuvre effect, it naturally balances 
out the intended effects amongst players. 
 
 
 
Cones, Lines, Radii and Sweeps 
Some attacks are not simply determined as one creature and one 
target and may instead hit a group of creatures. The terms Advanced 
Fantasy uses are explained below: 
 
Cones 
Some attacks and abilities, such as a red dragon’s breath or a gout 
of flame spell, affect a ‘cone’ of creatures. The number stated is the 
length the cone extends from the source, and it ends when it reaches 
the width stated. For example, a 15’ cone would look like this, with 
the wizard as the point of origin, assuming one ‘square’ on the area 
below is 5’: 
 
 
 
Lines 
Lines affect all creatures along a single trajectory and the listed 
distance is the length of the line. For example, a 20’ line with the 
wizard as the point of origin, assuming one ‘square’ on the area 
below is 5’ would look like this: 
 
 
 
Radius 
A radius affects a rounded area. A radius the size of the area from 
the centre point to the edge. For example, a 5’ radius on a 5’ grid 
would look like this: 
 
 
 
Sweep 
A sweep is a line directly adjacent to the creature and is often used 
when determining wide, cleaving swipes and arcs. If a creature can 
hit multiple targets with one melee attack sweep, then they must 
make an attack test against each. Two examples of a 15’ swipe with 
a fighter as the point of origin is shown below on the 5’ scale grid: 
 
 
 
 
 
 


Game Mastery 
105 
 
Conditions 
There are different conditions that the characters and other creatures 
can accrue because of various actions and injuries. They are 
explained below: 
▶ Bleeding: For every bleeding condition, a creature loses 1hp at 
the start of each round. Abilities that restore HP may also be used to 
remove bleeding conditions. In addition, any creature on 0 HP 
suffers a cumulative 10% chance of dying at the end of each round 
for each bleeding condition they have. The creature then saves vs 
END for each bleeding condition, removing one condition for each 
success. 
▶ Blinded: Creatures that are at a -9 to all tests that rely on sight. 
▶ Burning: A creature is on fire. For every burning condition, a 
creature loses 1hp at the start of each round. The creature then saves 
vs AGI for each burning condition, removing one condition for each 
success. 
▶ Drained: A creature takes a -1 to tests for each drained condition. 
A creature naturally loses two drained conditions at the end of an 
extended rest period. 
▶ Slowed: A slowed creature’s movement is reduced by half.  
▶ Stunned: A creature takes a -1 to all tests. A creature naturally 
loses one stunned condition at the end of the next round. 
 
Dual Wielding 
Sometimes a character may wish to use two weapons at once when 
fighting in melee. At least one of the weapons must have the 
Offhand weapon trait. This works as follows: 
 
▶ Attacks with the weapon in the main hand are made as normal. 
▶ Attacks with the off-hand weapon cost 1FP to use. 
▶ Attacks with the off-hand weapon are made at a -3 penalty. 
 
Dodging 
Sometimes a character may wish dodge an incoming attack. This is 
often better than taking the damage of very high-damage attacks 
such as those from rocket launchers and dragons’ breaths. Dodging 
works as follows: 
 
▶ Once the attacker has made a successful attack test, the defender 
may attempt a dodge test. This can be done against both melee and 
ranged attack tests. A success means the blow is dodged. A 
character may only dodge once per round. 
▶ If the defender fails their dodge test, the damage they would have 
suffered from the attack is increased by 3. 
 
Parrying 
Sometimes a character may wish to use their weapon to parry a 
blow. A weapon must have the Parry trait to do this. Parrying works 
as follows: 
 
▶ Once the attacker has made a successful attack test, the defender 
may attempt a melee test. If successful, the defender takes no 
damage. This can only be done against melee attacks. A character 
may only parry once per round. 
▶ If the defender fails their melee test, then they suffer a -1 to their 
Armour Saves. 
▶ A creature may only attempt to parry those attacks coming from a 
creature that is one size category larger than themselves or smaller. 
 
Blocking 
Sometimes a character may wish to use their shield to block a blow. 
A shield must have the Block trait to do this. Blocking works as 
follows: 
▶ Once the attacker has made a successful attack test, the defender 
may attempt a shield test. If successful, the defender takes no 
damage. This can be done against both melee and ranged attack 
tests. A character may only block once per round. 
▶ If the defender fails their shield test, then they suffer a -1 to their 
Armour Saves from the incoming attack. 
 
 
 
 
 
 
Suffocation and Drowning 
When a creature has its airflow cut off it is suffocating. 
 
When a creature is suffocating create a pool of d6s equal to the 
creature’s proficiency level in END. This is known as their 
suffocation pool. At the end of each round, they roll their 
suffocation pool of d6s and remove any natural 1s. Repeat this at 
the end of each round, removing the natural 1s each time. When the 
suffocation pool is totally depleted, the creature immediately drops 
to 0 hit points, is unconscious and considered to be dying. 
 
If a character has 0 proficiency levels in END (Untrained) then they 
still have a suffocation pool consisting of a single d4. 
 
Called Shots 
Sometimes a character may wish to shoot a particular part of their 
opponent, such as going straight for a head shot to kill a zombie or 
a leg strike to stop their opponent from fleeing into the night. We 
call these sorts of attacks ‘called shots’. Called shots work as follows:  
 
The attacker declares they are making a called shot, stating which 
part of the target they are aiming for. They also state how much of 
a penalty to the attack test they are willing to take in order to make 
the shot hit where they intend.  
 
If the attack hits, the character immediately rolls a d12 and adds the 
penalty they took on the attack test as a bonus to the d12 roll, 
consulting the table below that corresponds with the proficiency 
level of their attacking skill. This will determine if the intended 
location has been hit or not. 
 
For some results, there are multiple entries, this is to account for the 
different sorts of locations an attacker may be wishing to hit. 
Remember, the intended location of a called shot must be made 
before the attack test is made. If the attack test is successful but 
the d12 results in a different location to the one intended, and not 
enough of a penalty was accepted to shift the result to the correct 
location, then the attack is still successful, but it hits the main body 
of the target and damage is determined as usual, but the target gains 
a +1 to their armour saves against this attack..

Body/Tail/Legs/Feet/Arm/Hand/Head/Eyes/Underbelly 
12 
Body/Tail/Legs/Feet/Arm/Hand/Head/Eyes/Underbelly 
 
Unarmed Attacks 
When a character makes an unarmed attack, the damage is 1 
bludgeoning damage plus their Melee damage modifier (determined 
by their STR). However, a creature succeeds all Armour saves 
against this damage on a natural 4, 5 or 6, not just the usual 6. 
 
Siege and Lacerate Qualities 
Occasionally, a weapon with have the siege quality. This means that 
the weapon can deal damage to structure points of vehicles and 
buildings as well as normal to normal hp. 
 
The attacks of creatures that are huge or larger automatically have 
the siege quality. 
 
Often weapons that have the siege quality also have the lacerate 
quality. A weapon with the lacerate quality multiplies its damage by 
5 against creatures with hp (as opposed to buildings and vehicles 
with structure points)  
Morale 
Morale is a rule useful all types of campaigns, but especially in those 
that involve lots of combat and those which require sandbox 
emergent gameplay. Morale is a procedure that will help a game 
master decide when an NPC will attempt to leave a combat situation. 
 
Morale Tests 
Each creature will have a Morale rating listed in its statblock. To 
make a morale test, the game master simply rolls 1d20. The number 
rolled must be equal to or higher than the Morale rating to pass. On 
a failure the creature will attempt to surrender, flee or parley with 
the other combatants. If the creature passes its morale, then it will 
continue fighting. 
 
When to Test Morale 
It is ultimately up to the game master as to when Morale should be 
tested. However, here are a few principles to help guide your 
decision making. 
▶ 50% casualties: When the creature’s side has suffered 50% 
casualties, the game master should roll a Morale test. A failure 
indicates that the remaining combatants on that side should attempt 
to flee or surrender. 
▶ Leader Killed: When a leader on a creature’s side has been taken 
as a casualty, the game master should roll a Morale test. A failure 
indicates that the remaining combatants on that side should attempt 
to flee or surrender. 
▶ Lone Survivor: When a creature that was fighting as part of an 
enlarged group is the only active combatant left, , the game master 
should roll a Morale test. A failure indicates that the remaining 
combatant on that side should attempt to flee or surrender. 
 
Creatures with Different Morale Ratings 
Sometimes creatures in groups will have different Morale ratings, 
such as a group of bandits being led by a bandit captain. In these 
cases, if the game master is called upon to make a Morale test, then 
they should use the highest Morale rating amongst all the 
combatants on one side. 
 


Game Mastery 
107 
 
Experience 
Throughout the game, characters will gain experience points and 
levelling up. The manner in which characters gain experience can 
vary depending on the campaign. Below are a few general principles 
to help guide you. Characters should generally gain XP for the 
following: 
 
Defeating Foes 
Each creature a character may encounter will generally have an XP 
value listed in its statblock. This is the amount of XP the party should 
receive for defeating the monster. Note, that defeating does not 
necessarily mean ‘killing’. Sometimes a defeated monster may have 
surrendered or fled. 
 
Interesting Ideas 
The game master should award XP if a player comes up with a 
particularly smart solution to a problem that is present. Use the 
miscellaneous XP table to help you work this out. 
 
Overcoming Other Obstacles 
From locating the entrance to the ruins, to negotiating a difficult 
meeting with a noble, there are many encounters and events that are 
deserving of experience points. A couple of less well-defined reasons 
a game master may wish to reward experience points for are: 
▶ Social Encounters: Encounters where characters have to 
negotiate, persuade or intimidate NPCs might offer experience if the 
game master feels that the encounter should be justly rewarded 
(simply paying the tollkeeper to get into a city should not generate 
XP rewards). 
▶ Investigation: Encounters where characters must investigate or 
otherwise solve some sort of puzzle may warrant an XP reward. 
▶ Exploration: The discovery of major locations such as dungeon 
sites and settlements may warrant an XP reward. 
▶Quest or Goal Completion: Completing a major quest (or even a 
minor) one, or part thereof should be rewarded with XP. 
▶Good Roleplaying: A character whose player leans into their 
motivations or beliefs, actively playing out that character’s alignment 
should be rewarded. 
 
For each of these types of encounters, game masters may refer to 
the Miscellaneous Experience Rewards table below.

Game Mastery 
108 
 
Encounters
Encounters are the bread and butter of adventure. This section 
contains rules and procedures for generating encounters as the 
characters travel the game world, both overland and deep in the 
dungeon. 
 
Wilderness Encounters 
Most characters in a game of Advanced Fantasy will spend time 
walking from location to location, often through areas of unexplored 
wilderness. 
 
Movement in the Wilderness 
Every character has a movement rate per day. The slowest member 
of a party determines the overall speed of the party, unless the 
members of the group are happy to leave one or more of their slower 
companions behind.  
 
Some terrain will affect the movement speed of the party, as 
determined by the terrain types below: 
▶ Desert: 100% speed/Challenging (+0) Survival to avoid getting 
lost each day. 
▶ Barren, Forest, Hill: 75% speed/Hard (-3) Survival to avoid 
getting lost each day. 
▶ Jungle, Mountain, Swamp: 50% speed/Hard (-6) Survival to 
avoid getting lost each day. 
▶ Plain/Scrub: 100% speed/Easy (+3) Survival to avoid getting lost 
each day. 
▶ Road: 150% speed/no survival test required to avoid getting lost. 
 
Wilderness Encounter Die 
Each day that a party is exploring the wilderness, roll two dice to 
see if they get an encounter during the day, or at night. If the dice 
comes up as a ‘1’ then they have an encounter. The dice rolled for 
this are determined by the current terrain, as seen below: 
▶ Desert, Mountain: Daytime d8; Nighttime d12. 
▶ Barren, Forest, Hill, Plain, Scrub: Daytime d6; Nighttime d12. 
▶ Jungle, Swamp: Daytime d6; Nighttime d8. 
▶ Settled Terrain*: Daytime d6; Nighttime d10. 
 
*Any terrain within 12 miles of a large town, city or fortification that 
has the power to exert its influence over the region through the use 
of patrols and other measures. 
 
Wilderness Encounter Distance 
If an encounter occurs, then the game master rolls 3d6 and multiplies 
the result by ten. This is the number of feet away that the 
approaching creature(s) is when someone in the party detects it. For 
nighttime encounters, only multiply the number by 5, unless 
appropriate watches have been set. 
 
 
 
 
 
Wilderness Encounter Tables 
To help a game master adjudicate what creature is met when an encounter is generated, roll on the table below, appropriate for the party’s current 
terrain type.

Game Mastery 
111 
 
Adventure Site Encounters 
Not all encounters will take place in the wilderness while exploring 
or travelling, many instead will take place within an adventure site 
such as a dungeon, ruined fort, labyrinthine woods or other location.  
 
Movement in Adventure Sites 
While in adventure sites characters use their movement per turn 
rating. This rating is calculated by taking their Movement/Round, 
multiplying it by five. The reason it is not multiplied by ten (as there 
are ten rounds to a turn) is because outside of combat characters are 
likely to be walking slowly, checking for traps and moving 
cautiously. 
 
Adventure Site Encounter Die 
The game master should check to see if a party has an encounter 
when in adventure sites, as determined by the information below. 
Roll the dice at the appropriate time. If the result is a ‘1’ then an 
encounter occurs: 
▶ Adventure site such as a dungeon: 1d6 every two turns (20 
minutes). 
 
Adventure Site Encounter Distance 
If an encounter occurs, then the game master rolls 1d6 and multiplies 
the result by ten. This is the number of feet away that the 
approaching creature(s) is when someone in the party detects it. For 
nighttime encounters or those in total darkness, only multiply the 
number by 5. 
 
Dungeon Encounter Tables 
To help a game master adjudicate what creature is met when an 
encounter is generated, roll on the table below if an encounter 
occurs in a dungeon. It may be that a particular adventure has its 
own random encounter tables, in such a case the game master 
should use those instead.  
 
The below tables are for each level of a dungeon. The first level of 
a dungeon should generally be appropriate for the questing of low-
level and novice adventurers, while the fifth level is only for the most 
competent and experienced. The second, third and fourth level 
represent the rising challenges between the first and fifth. All results 
use the Encounter value on the number appearing in a monsters 
statblock.

Game Mastery 
113 
 
Waterborne Encounters 
Adventuring about bodies of water presents its own thrills and 
challenges: the environment itself the means in which combat is 
perpetrated have the potential to be wildly different from those 
creatures and events encountered on dry land.  
 
Movement on Water 
The use of marine vehicles like ships and boats is not covered here, 
but in the chapter detailing vehicles and structures. The movement 
speed of a particular sailing vessel will be largely down to factors 
such as size, adequate crew and favourable currents and winds. 
 
Waterborne Encounter Die 
The game master should check to see if a party has an encounter 
when adventuring on or near water, as determined by the 
information below. Roll the dice at the appropriate time. If the result 
is a ‘1’ then an encounter occurs: 
▶ Lake, River: Daytime d6; Nighttime d12. 
▶ Ocean, Sea: Daytime d8; Nighttime d12 
 
Waterborne Encounter Tables 
The tables below are used to help a game master adjudicate what 
creatures are encountered when a party traverses the waterways, 
seas and oceans of the world. 
Waterborne Encounter Distance 
If an encounter occurs, then the game master rolls 3d6 and multiplies 
the result by ten. This is the number of yards away that the 
approaching creature(s) is when someone in the party detects it. For 
nighttime encounters, only multiply the number by 5, unless 
appropriate watches have been set.

Game Mastery 
114 
 
Evasion 
Sometimes one party in a fight may wish to flee and the other may 
wish to pursue. In these cases, a game master may use to following 
rules to quickly determine if the fleeing party escapes or not. During 
evasive action, it is assumed both sides are running as quickly as 
they can.

If a creature or party attempts an evasion test and fails, then the 
pursue catch up and threaten to overrun the pursued. Each member 
of the pursued party gains a drained condition in such a situation. 
Reaction Rolls 
Reaction rolls are used to help a game master decide how NPCs and 
wild creatures respond to the presence of characters. For example, 
this could be whether a dragon tries to kill a character or parley with 
a party first. It could, however, also be how a sage responds to the 
characters’ requests for help. A reaction roll is not always required – 
sometimes the contents of an adventure or the characters’ previous 
actions will overrule the need for a reaction roll. To make a reaction 
roll, roll on the following table: 
 
REACTION ROLL TABLE 
2d6 
Result 
2 
Critical Failure: The creature or NPC is incensed by 
the characters. It will do everything it can to harm 
them, both immediately and in the future. 
3-4 
Hostile: The creature or NPC feels a strong enmity 
against the characters. If it is socially acceptable (and 
sometimes even if it isn’t) then it will attack.  
5 
Threatening: The creature or NPC is generally hostile 
to the characters. It will retest next round with a -5 
penalty unless the characters do something to change 
this. 
6-8 
Cautious: The creature is unsure how to respond. It 
may attack, help or flee, but its response should be 
based on the character’s immediate actions. Retest 
again next round. 
9 
Curious: The creature or NPC is cautiously curious 
about the characters. It will retest next round with a +5 
bonus unless the characters do something to 
significantly change this. 
10-11 
Friendly: The creature or NPC feels a strong sense of 
affability towards the characters. If it is socially 
acceptable then it will generally do what it can to help 
the characters. 
12 
Critical Success: The creature or NPC feels that the 
characters have entered its life at just the right time. 
Both immediately and in the future, it will do its best to 
aid the characters however it can. 
 
Modifiers to Reaction Rolls 
Whoever is the main character interacting with a particular creature 
(and we must appreciate as game masters that this may change 
from round to round), then certain modifiers based on PER come 
into play. A character should gain a bonus to reaction roll equal to 
their proficiency level in PER. 
 
 


Game Mastery 
115 
 
Vehicles and Structures 
Vehicles and structures will play a significant role in many 
campaigns by game masters as they cover everything from dwarfen 
steam machines to northmen longships, from mud huts to castle 
walls.  
 
Structure Points 
Structure points (abbreviated as SP) replace hit points for vehicles 
and structures. A structure point is not harmed by regular damage – 
it is only harmed by attacks which have the siege special rule, 
ignoring the damage otherwise. 
 
Huge and Larger Creatures 
Creatures that are of huge size or large may automatically give any 
of their attacks the siege quality. 
 
Combat Involving Vehicles 
Combat involving vehicles works just like ordinary combat with a 
few caveats. 
 
Vehicular Combat Scale 
It is quite possible that a game master would like to have scenarios 
where there are many vehicles involved, and the primary form of 
combat is between these vehicles. This may be the case in situations 
where player characters are part of a pirate crew on the high seas 
for instance, and battling other privateers and pirate hunters forms 
a basis for the campaign. 
 
Conduct vehiclear combat on a hexagonal map with each hex 
equalling 10’. This matches the drift and manoeuvre values most 
closely. This is a deep abstraction, and on a combat map, each 
vehicle should only take up 1 hex.  
 
Vehicle Damage 
When a vehicle suffers unsaved damage from an attack with the 
siege special rule, add all the unsaved damage into one pool and 
consult the table below for what kind of impact has been made: 
 
VEHICLE HIT TABLE 
Damage 
Result 
1-3 
1 single impact 
4-6 
2 single impacts 
7-9 
1 double impact 
10-12 
2 double impacts 
13-15 
1 triple impact 
16+ 
2 triple impacts 
 
Once the types of impacts have been made, roll 1d12 on the table 
below for each impact. You roll once for each impact (regardless of 
whether it is single, double, or triple). A double impact counts as 
two impacts to the listed location, and a triple impact counts as 
three. 
 
VEHICLE HIT TABLE 
1d12 
Impact Location 
1 
Structure 
2 
Crew 
3 
Structure 
4 
Armour 
5 
Structure 
6 
Power Source 
7 
Structure 
8 
Locomotion 
9 
Weapon 
10 
Locomotion 
11 
Weapon 
12 
Attacker’s Choice 
Armour 
The vehicle’s armour is reduced by 1. If it becomes 6+, then all 
future impacts on this location are against the structure. 
 
 
 
Crew 
This means a member of the crew has been hit. Determine randomly 
who has been hit for each level of impact (single, double, or triple) 
the crew member suffers 5 points of damage of the type originally 
used to attack the vehicle. If there are no crew, then all impacts on 
this location are against the structure. 
 
Locomotion 
The manner in which the vehicle moves has been hit.  
▶First Impact: The vehicle’s speed is reduced by 50%. 
▶Second Impact: The vehicle’s speed is reduced by 75%. 
▶Third Impact: The vehicle’s speed is reduced by 100%. 
▶Further Impacts: Count these as being against the structure. 
 
Power Source 
The mode of powering the vehicle has been hit. 
▶First Impact: The vehicle’s speed is reduced by 25% 
▶Second Impact: All creatures within the vehicle make tests with a 
-3 penalty so long as the vehicle is in motion. 
▶Third Impact: The vehicle’s power supply is destroyed. The 
vehicle immediately suffers one additional single impact. 
▶Further Impacts: Count these as being against the structure. 
 
Structure 
Reduce the vehicle’s SP by 1-3, depending on the impact type. 
▶ Single Impact: Reduce the vehicle or structure’s SP by 1. 
▶ Double Impact: Reduce the vehicle or structure’s SP by 2. 
▶ Triple Impact: Reduce the vehicle or structure’s SP by 3. 
 
Weapon 
If there are multiple weapons on the vehicle, determine randomly 
which is impacted. 
▶First Impact: Attacks by the weapon suffer a -3 penalty to their 
attack tests. 
▶Second Impact: Attacks by the weapon suffer a -6 penalty to their 
attack tests. 
▶Third Impact: The weapon is destroyed. 
▶Further Impacts: Count these as being against the structure. 
 
Weapon Batteries 
Some vehicles may have several weapons of the same type, such as 
a battleship having 30 torpedo tubes. To make these easier to run, a 
game master may merge multiple weapons into a single ‘battery’. By 
doing this, the battery multiplies its damage by the number of 
individual weapons in the battery. The number of crew required to 
operate the battery should also be multiplied by the number of 
individual weapons. A single attack test is made to fire a battery. 
 
Vehicle Statistics 
A vehicle entry contains a number of abbreviations that are defined 
below: 
▶SP: Structure Points. This represents the structural integrity of a 
vehicle. When it reaches 0, the vehicle is destroyed. 
▶AS: Armour Save. It tells you what the vehicle’s armour save is. If 
there is a second save listed, such as 5+/6+ then the second save is 
a power save. 
▶Move: How far the vehicle can move in the move step. 
▶ Drift: The minimum amount of movement a vehicle must go in a 
straight line before it can make a turn. 
▶ Manoeuvre: The maximum number of degrees a vehicle can turn 
before needing to move its drift value again. 
▶ Size: There are four different categories of size: medium, large, 
huge and gargantuan Medium vehicles take up 5’x5’ space. Large 
vehicles take up a 10’x10’ space. Huge vehicles take up 15’x15’ 
space. Gargantuan vehicles take up to or more than a 20’x20’ space. 
▶ Immune: If the vehicle is hit with this damage type, then the 
vehicle takes no damage. 
▶ Abilities: Lists other abilities that a vehicle may have. 
 
 


Game Mastery 
116 
 
Boarding Actions 
If a boarding action between two vehicles such as ships occurs, roll 
1d100 for each side. Add the number of attackers and defenders 
(usually the crew) as bonuses to their respective rolls and add the 
level of any character’s present as a bonus too. The highest number 
wins.  
 
A defender pushes the enemy ship off whereas an attacker gains 
control of much (if not all) of the ship they have boarded. This is 
very abstract but can be useful if game masters need a resolution to 
this sort of activity quickly. 
 
If a more thorough outcome is needed, use the Battle Engine or War 
Engine rules and calculate both crews as the relevant forces. 
 
Structures 
Fighting and damaging structures works exactly like fighting and 
damaging vehicles, only count every hit as against the structure. 
 
The following gives the amount of structure points and armour 
typical of several structures.

Vehicles 
Below are some vehicles that may be present in a campaign of 
Advanced Fantasy. 
 
Barge – Huge Vehicle 
Barges ply their trades on the rivers and lakes of the world. 
SP: 12  AS: 5+  Move: sail 40’/4mph  Drift: 10’  Manoeuvre: 120° 
Armour: 
▶ The vehicle has reinforced wooden boards, giving a 5+ armour 
save. 
Crew: 6 + 12 passengers 
▶ 6 crew members are required to move and drive the ship. The 
skill to do this is Knowledge (Sailing). Additionally, these crew may 
defend the ship with their weapons. 
▶ 12 passenger slots are available. 
Locomotion:  
▶ The ship is moved by its oarsmen. 
 
Power Source:  
▶ The ship’s power source is its hull. 
 
Weapons: 
▶ The vehicle has no weapons but can give up 15 tons of cargo 
space to put a siege weapon on deck. 
Abilities:  
▶ Flammable: The ship is particularly at risk to fire. Any fire damage 
the ship suffers counts as having the siege quality. 
▶ Cargo: The barge may carry 50 tons of cargo. 
 
Canoe – Huge Vehicle 
Canoes can be used to traverse marshy areas and slow-moving rivers. 
SP: 3  AS: 6+  Move: 30’/3mph Drift: 10’  Manoeuvre: 180° 
Armour: 
▶ The vehicle has no armour. 
Crew: 1 + 5 Passengers 
▶ 1 crew member is required to move and drive the vehicle. The 
attribute to do this is Strength. 
▶ 6 passenger slots are available. 
Locomotion:  
▶ The ship is moved by its oarsmen. 
 
Power Source:  
▶ The ship’s power source is its hull. 
 
Weapons: 
▶ The vehicle has no weapons. 
Abilities:  
▶ Flammable: The ship is particularly at risk to fire. Any fire damage 
the ship suffers counts as having the siege quality. 
▶ Open Topped: Attacks against this vehicle may be made against 
the crew and passengers instead of the vehicle.  
 
Carriage – Huge Vehicle 
Carriages are carts with enclosed roofs to protect passengers from the 
elements of the world. 
SP: 5  AS: 5+  Move: 30’/3mph Drift: 10’  Manoeuvre: 120° 
Armour: 
▶ The vehicle has reinforced wooden boards, giving a 5+ armour 
save. 
Crew: 1 + 6 Passengers 
▶ 1 crew member is required to move and drive the vehicle. The 
skill to do this is Animal Handling. 
▶ 4 passenger slots are available. 
Locomotion:  
▶ The vehicle’s locomotion is 4 wooden wheels. 
 
Power Source:  
▶ The vehicle must be pulled by at least 1 beast of burden. If the 
impact location is the power source, ignore the normal damage. 
Instead deal damage to one of the creature’s pulling the carriage. 
▶ If at least four large creatures are pulling the carriage, increase its 
Move to 40’ (providing the animals can actually move at least this 
quickly usually). 
Weapons: 
▶ The vehicle has no weapons.  
 
Cart – Huge Vehicle 
Common in many fantasy settings, the simple horse and cart is a 
useful vehicle when attempting long voyages. 
SP: 3  AS: 6+  Move: 30’/3mph Drift: 10’  Manoeuvre: 120° 
Armour: 
▶ The vehicle has no armour. 
Crew: 1 + 6 Passengers 
▶ 1 crew member is required to move and drive the vehicle. The 
skill to do this is Animal Handling. 
▶ 6 passenger slots are available. 
Locomotion:  
▶ The vehicle’s locomotion is 4 wooden wheels. 
 
Power Source:  
▶ The vehicle must be pulled by at least 1 beast of burden. If the 
impact location is the power source, ignore the normal damage. 
Instead deal damage to one of the creature’s pulling the cart. 
▶ If at least four large creatures are pulling the cart, increase its 
Move to 40’ (providing the animals can actually move at least this 
quickly usually). 
Weapons: 
▶ The vehicle has no weapons. 
Abilities:  
▶ Open Topped: Attacks against this vehicle may be made against 
the crew and passengers instead of the vehicle.

Chariot – Large Vehicle 
Chariots have been used in war since antiquity, to run down foes 
and encircle infantry. 
SP: 4  AS: 5+  Move: 30’/3mph Drift: 5’  Manoeuvre: 120° 
Armour: 
▶ The vehicle has reinforced wooden boards. 
Crew: 1 + 2 Passengers 
▶ 1 crew member is required to move and drive the vehicle. The 
skill to do this is Animal Handling. 
▶ 2 passenger slots are available. 
Locomotion:  
▶ The vehicle’s locomotion is 2 wooden wheels. 
 
Power Source:  
▶ The vehicle must be pulled by at least 1 beast of burden. If the 
impact location is the power source, ignore the normal damage. 
Instead deal damage to one of the creature’s pulling the chariot. 
▶ If at least two large creatures are pulling the chariot, increase its 
Move to 50’ (providing the animals can actually move at least this 
quickly usually). 
Weapons: 
▶ The vehicle has no weapons.  
Abilities:  
▶ Open Topped: Attacks against this vehicle may be made against 
the crew and passengers instead of the vehicle.  
 
Cog – Gargantuan Vehicle 
Cogs are reliable trading ships sometimes used in war. 
SP: 12  AS: 4+  Move: sail 80’/8mph  Drift: 20’  Manoeuvre: 60° 
Armour: 
▶ The vehicle has a reinforced hull plating giving a 4+ armour save. 
Crew: 20 + 20 passengers 
▶ 20 crew members are required to move and drive the ship. The 
skill to do this is Knowledge (Sailing). Additionally, these crew may 
defend the ship with their weapons. 
▶ 20 passenger slots are available. 
Locomotion:  
▶ The ship is moved by its sail. 
 
Power Source:  
▶ The ship’s power source is its hull. 
 
Weapons: 
The cog has two scorpions. 
▶ Scorpion: Ranged; 3 piercing, Lacerate, Reload (3), Rend (3), 
Penetrate (10’) Unwieldy, Range (300’). 
 
▶ Scorpion: Ranged; 3 piercing, Lacerate, Reload (3), Rend (3), 
Penetrate (10’) Unwieldy, Range (300’). 
 
Abilities:  
▶ Flammable: The ship is particularly at risk to fire. Any fire damage 
the ship suffers counts as having the siege quality. 
▶ Sail-Driven: The ship doubles its speed when moving directly 
with the wind. It halves its speed when directly against it. 
▶ Cargo: The cog may carry 200 tons of cargo. 
 
 
 
 
 
 
Dwarfen Airship – Gargantuan Vehicle 
The pinnacle of dwarfen engineering: an airship resembles a brass-
armoured zeppelin bristling with cannon. 
SP: 22  AS: 4+  Move: fly 80’/8mph  Drift: 20’  Manoeuvre: 60° 
Armour: 
▶ The vehicle has brass plating giving a 4+ armour save. 
Crew: 66 + 20 passengers 
▶ 6 crew members are required to move and drive the vehicle. The 
skill to do this is Knowledge (Engineering) 
▶ 16 crew members are required to work on the engineering deck 
in order to keep the location and power source working. 
▶ 3 crew members are required for each dwarfen stub cannon. (6 
for each battery) 
▶ 20 additional crew are on standby in case their fellows are taken 
as casualties. 
▶ 20 passenger slots are available. 
Locomotion:  
The vehicle has two large sets of thrusters, whose damage is tracked 
independently. Both must suffer damage for the vehicle to suffer the 
effects of locomotion impacts. 
▶ Set of thrusters. 
 
▶ Set of thrusters. 
 
Power Source:  
▶ The vehicle’s power source is a series of steam engines. 
 
Weapons: 
The dwarfen stub cannons are merged into batteries of two 
individual guns each. 
▶ Two Dwarfen Stub Cannons: [Recharge 5+]: Ranged; 6 
piercing, rend (2), siege, lacerate. 
 
▶ Two Dwarfen Stub Cannons: [Recharge 5+]: Ranged; 6

▶ Two Dwarfen Stub Cannons: [Recharge 5+]: Ranged; 6

Howdah – Large Vehicle 
Howdahs are large saddle-like scaffolds attached to the backs of 
elephants, allowing a crew to attack creatures below. 
SP: 4  AS: 5+  Move: as creature Drift: 0’  Manoeuvre: 360° 
Armour: 
▶ The vehicle has reinforced wooden boards, giving a 5+ armour 
save. 
Crew: 1 + 4 Passengers 
▶ 1 crew member is required to move and drive the vehicle. The 
skill to do this is Animal Handling. 
▶ 4 passenger slots are available. 
Locomotion:  
▶ If the impact location is the locomotion, ignore the normal 
damage. Instead deal damage to the elephant. 
Power Source:  
▶ The vehicle must be placed on top of at least 1 elephant. If the 
impact location is the power source, ignore the normal damage. 
Instead deal damage to the elephant. 
Weapons: 
▶ The vehicle has no weapons.  
Abilities:  
▶ Open Topped: Attacks against this vehicle may be made against 
the crew and passengers instead of the vehicle.  
 
Longship – Gargantuan Vehicle 
Longships are fast moving ships favoured by northmen raiders and 
other barbarians. 
SP: 14  AS: 5+  Move: sail 120’/12mph  Drift: 20’  Manoeuvre: 120° 
Armour: 
▶ The vehicle has reinforced wooden boards, giving a 5+ armour 
save. 
Crew: 25  
▶ 25 crew members are required to move and drive the ship. The 
skill to do this is Knowledge (Sailing). Additionally, these crew may 
defend the ship with their weapons. 
Locomotion:  
▶ The ship is moved by its sail. 
 
Power Source:  
▶ The ship’s power source is its hull. 
 
Weapons: 
▶ The vehicle has no weapons. 
Abilities:  
▶ Flammable: The ship is particularly at risk to fire. Any fire damage 
the ship suffers counts as having the siege quality. 
▶ Sail-Driven: The ship doubles its speed when moving directly 
with the wind. It halves its speed when directly against it. 
▶ Cargo: The longship may carry 24 tons of cargo. 
 
Raft – Large Vehicle 
Rafts are used to get from one place to another on seas in a pinch. 
SP: 5  AS: 6+  Move: sail 30’/3mph  Drift: 10’  Manoeuvre: 60° 
Armour: 
▶ The vehicle has no armour. 
▶ 1 crew member is required to move and drive the vehicle. The 
attribute to do this is Strength. 
▶ 6 passenger slots are available. 
Locomotion:  
▶ The ship is moved by its sail. 
 
Power Source:  
▶ The ship’s power source is its hull. 
 
Weapons: 
▶ The vehicle has no weapons. 
Abilities:  
▶ Flammable: The ship is particularly at risk to fire. Any fire damage 
the ship suffers counts as having the siege quality. 
▶ Sail-Driven: The ship doubles its speed when moving directly 
with the wind. It halves its speed when directly against it. 
▶ Open Topped: Attacks against this vehicle may be made against 
the crew and passengers instead of the vehicle.  
 
 
 
 
 
 
 


Game Mastery 
119 
 
Mass Combat 
In many games it will be necessary at some time to adjudicate the 
combat of dozens, hundreds or even millions of individuals. JEM 
provides two possible systems for this type of combat that game 
masters can utilise. 
 
The Battle Engine 
The Battle Engine is a set of rules that allows a game master run 
exciting battles with lots of choices on both sides of the conflict. It 
is best utilised in situations where the game master wishes the 
players to have individual choices regarding the placement of 
troops and the specific attacks and actions of individual units of 
soldiery.  
 
Units 
Within a Battle Engine combat, all combatants are organised into 
units, such as ‘40 orcs’  or ‘10,000 marines’  and the statistics are 
calculated from the base creature as follows: 
 
▶ 1. Calculate Unit HP: Take the creature’s original HP and multiply 
it by the number of creatures in the unit. Then divide the total by 
the size modifier of the creatures in the unit, as stated below: 
 
Unit Hit Point Size Modifier 
▶ Tiny: divide by 5 
▶ Small, Medium and Large: divide by 4 
▶ Huge: divide by 3 
▶ Gargantuan: divide by 2 
 
▶ 2. Calculate Unit Battle Damage: Take the creature’s original 
damage for each of its attacks and multiply it by the number of 
creatures in the unit. Then divide the total by the size modifier of 
the creatures in the unit, as stated below: 
 
Unit Damage Size Modifier 
▶ Tiny: divide by 30 
▶ Small, Medium and Large: divide by 20 
▶ Huge: divide by 5 
▶ Gargantuan: divide by 2 
 
▶ 3. Calculate Unit Wavering Limit: When a unit in a Battle Engine 
combat has lost half of its starting hit points it is wavering. A 
wavering unit only causes half of its original battle damage until it 
has left the battle and has been restored to full fighting strength. 
This can be represented by the WV initials in the top line of the 
statblock.  
 
▶ 4. Calculate Unit Speed: In a Battle Engine combat, each round 
becomes 10 minutes long. To calculate the speed of a unit, multiply 
its usual speed by 10. 
 
▶ 5. Calculate Power of Other Abilities: Some abilities, such as a 
troll’s ability to regenerate of a priest’s ability to cast cure wounds 
need to be upscaled to see the benefit of it. In this case, multiply the 
original number by the level of the creature with it.  
 
Area of Effect Attacks 
Sometimes a creature has an area of effect attack, such as a 
dragon’s breath. In these cases, calculate the number of medium 
sized creatures this attack could affect, and multiply the base 
damage by this to find the battle damage. Don’t forget to apply the 
Unit Damage Size Modifier. 
Units With Mixed Creatures 
Sometimes a unit may contain mixed types of creatures. A unit with 
mixed creatures may still only take one action per round. 
▶ HP: When calculating HP, simply calculate the Unit HP for the 
different creatures then add them together.  
▶ Damage: Calculate the damage for each of the creatures’ attacks 
and list them separately in the statblock. 
▶ AS: The unit’s armour save becomes the save of the majority of 
the creatures. So, a unit with 700 orcs with an armour save of 5+, 
accompanied by 100 knights with an armour save of 2+ would have 
a unit armour save of 5+. 
▶ Morale: Becomes equal to the best morale value of all creatures 
in the unit. 
▶ Movement: Becomes the speed of the slowest moving creature in 
the unit. 
▶ Saves: If a unit is called upon to make a save, use the core 
attribute of the most common creature in the group.  
 
Units with Player Characters 
Obviously, it is possible that player characters will join units. If this 
is done, then the unit is modified in the following way: 
▶ HP: Add the character’s HP directly to the unit’s HP.  
▶ Damage: A character may add their level as bonus damage to one 
attack the unit makes per round. 
▶ AS: The unit’s armour save is unchanged by the presence of a 
player character. 
▶ Morale: The unit gains a +1 bonus to morale tests for each player 
character present. 
▶ Actions: Each round, character present may take an action instead 
of the unit taking an action, such a character casting cure wounds 
on his comrades or casting fireball into an advancing unit of orcs. 
 
Example 1: 1,000 Orcs 
Below is an example of a statblock created using 1,000 orcs. 
 
1,000 Orcs 
HP: 500  WV: 250  AS: 5+ (boiled leather)  Morale: 10  Move: 300’ 
STR: J (12); AGI: U (15); END: T (13); INT: U (15); PER: U (15) 
Skills: Melee: J (12); Ranged: T (13); Stealth: J (12) 
Actions:  
▶ Scimitar Attack: Melee (12); 150 slashing. 
▶ Javelin Attack: Ranged (12); 100 piercing (80’ range). 
 
Example 2: A Mixed Unit with Bandits and Dragon 
Below is an example of a statblock created using 100 bandits and 1 
young dragon. 
 
It may not seem too helpful to include the dragon, but when one 
considered its breath attack and the significant increase in morale it 
gives, then the benefits are more obvious. 
 
100 Bandits and Young Dragon 
HP: 54  WV: 27 AS: 5+ (studded leather)  Morale: 6  Move: 300’ 
Bandits: Skills, Actions and Abilities 
STR: T (13); AGI: T (13); END: U (15); INT: U (15); PER: T (13) 
Skills: Melee: T (13); Ranged: T (13);  Stealth: J (12) 
Young Dragon: Skills, Actions and Abilities 
STR: E (9); AGI: E (9); END: E (9); INT: E (9); PER: E (9) 
Skills: Melee: M (8); Ranged: E (9) 
Actions:  
▶ Shortsword Attack: Melee (13); 15 slashing. 
▶ Shortbow Attack: Ranged (13);  15 piercing (120’ range). 
▶ 2 x Claws Attack; 1 x Bite: Melee (8); 1/1 slashing; Melee (10); 
1/1 piercing, rend (2). 
▶ Flaming Breath: [Recharge 5+]: A young dragon exudes a 25’ 
cone of fire. All creatures in the cone take 15 fire damage (a save vs 
AGI halves the damage). 
Dramatic Actions: 1/round 
▶ 1 x Claws Attack: Melee (8); 1 slashing. 
▶ 1 x Tail Whip Attack: Melee (8); 1 bludgeoning, and the target 
must save vs STR or be knocked 20’ from the young dragon. 


Game Mastery 
120 
 
Combat in the Battle Engine 
Combat takes place in a number of rounds during a battle engine 
conflict. However, unlike the usual combat round, one usually 
represents 10 minutes. A game master may decide to increase this, 
to one hour, one day or even more. Just remember that in doing 
so, you must increase the calculated movement rate of units if you 
do this. 
 
The Battle Engine Round 
A Battle Engine round is broken into the following Steps each round: 
 
Combat Steps Each Round 
1. Declare Actions Step 
2. Initiative Step 
3. Move Step 
4. Missile Attacks Step. 
5. Supernatural Abilities Step 
6. Melee Attacks Step 
7. Other Actions Step 
 
1. Declare Actions Step 
During this step all the players declare what their units intend to do 
that round, such as ‘I will send my heavy infantry up into those hills’ 
or ‘I will hang back and try and shoot at those advancing orcs from 
the castle walls’. While these descriptions can be fairly broad, the 
game master should insist on a few things: attacks must be declared 
as either Missile or Melee as well as the intended target; spells being 
cast must be specified (it is not enough to declare ‘I will cast a spell’ 
but the spell to be cast must be stated); all supernatural abilities must 
be declared. 
 
2. Initiative Step 
In this step, the players roll 1d6 and the game master rolls 1d6 for 
the enemies. The highest roll resolves their declared actions first 
(Steps 3 to 7), then the losing side does the same. A tie should be 
rerolled unless a character as a talent that affects this. 
 
3. Move Step 
Each unit may move up to its listed move value. 
 
4. Missile Attacks Step 
Ranged weapons can now be used, such a bows and throwing 
daggers. 
 
5. Supernatural Abilities Step 
This is when units use special actions that are supernatural. This 
might be a priest’s Banish Undead ability or a wizard casting Bolt. 
 
6. Melee Attacks Step 
Melee weapons may now be used, such as swords and pikes. It is 
also when natural weapons such as claws are most commonly used. 
 
7. Other Actions Step 
This is when actions such as drinking potions, reloading a character’s 
revolver or trying to close the blast doors are resolved. A unit that 
took no other action may also move again, up to their listed move 
value. 
 
 
 
 
Attacks and Damage 
Below are the rules for making attacks and damaging opponents in 
a Battle Engine combat. 
 
Making an Attack in the Battle Engine 
If a unit has declared they are making an attack, then in the 
appropriate Combat Step they are able to do so. Resolve using the 
following: 
 
1. Make a Challenging (+0) test with the appropriate skill for the 
unit’s weapon (which is listed in the weapon entry) This is called 
the attack test. For example, for attacks with a longsword for 
example, this is the Melee skill. 
 
2. If the attack test is a success, then the attack has scored a hit, 
causing damage. If it has missed, then it causes half its usual damage. 
 
3. If the attack is a success, roll 1d6. If the roll meets or exceeds the 
armour save value of the unit, that damage is ignored. Remove the  
hit points for each failed armour save. Unlike normal combat, in the 
battle engine, you do not roll for each point of damage caused, 
instead one d6 is rolled for the entire damage and it is either 
deflected or not.  
 
A natural roll of a 6 means that the attack has been stopped, 
regardless of any other modifiers. If a natural roll of 1 is made, then 
the damage is applied regardless of any other modifiers. 
 
Rend and Power Saves 
Many weapons and creature attacks will list a Rend value, such as 
Rend (1). This is a negative penalty applied to armour saves 
(including cover saves). For example, if a unit has a 4+ armour save 
and is struck by an attack that has Rend (1) then they would need 
to roll a 5+ to succeed. Remember, a natural 6 on an armour save 
always succeeds, regardless of other modifiers (and a natural 1 
always fails). 
 
Sometimes a creature will have access to a power save. This is a 
special type of save that works exactly like armour saves and is 
rolled in addition to armour saves. Any failed armour saves may 
be rerolled if the creature has a power save. Nothing ever modifies 
a power save, under any circumstances and therefore power saves 
cannot be affected by something like the Rend weapon quality. 
 
Resistances and Vulnerabilities 
▶ Resistance: If a creature has resistance to a damage type then 
any damage it takes from an attack is halved, to a minimum of one. 
For example, if a creature with resistance to slashing damage is hit 
for two points of slashing from a longsword, then one point of 
damage is ignored automatically. The creature then rolls its armour 
saves against the remaining damage. 
▶ Vulnerability: If a creature has vulnerability to a damage type 
then any damage it takes from an attack is doubled. For example, if 
a creature with vulnerability to slashing damage is hit for two 
points of slashing from a longsword, then it must make its armour 
saves against four points of damage. 
 
Wavering, Morale and Destruction 
When a unit is reduced to its wavering limit, it must immediately half 
the value of its damage. In addition, it immediately makes a Morale 
test or breaks and flees as soon as it is able to do so. 
 
Saves 
Oftentimes a unit is called upon to make a save. This is almost 
always to avoid the effects of something unpleasant, such as a spell 
or trap. This will be stated as a ‘save vs’ and then a stated attribute, 
such as a ‘save vs PER’. A save is simply a test taken for that core 
attribute.  
 
Critical Hits 
If the attack roll is a natural 20 then the hit scored is ‘critical’. The 
damage from the attack is doubled. 
 


Game Mastery 
121 
 
Cover 
Sometimes a unit is in cover, granting an extra save to reduce 
damage if an attack hits. This save is taken before any armour saves 
or power saves and is referred to as a cover save. A cover save is 
affected by anything that affects an armour save and similarly 
always succeeds on a natural 6 and fails on a natural 1. 
 
To benefit from a cover save, a unit must be at least 50% hidden 
from the would-be attackers. Similarly, in most circumstances a unit 
only gains the benefit of a cover save during the Missile Attacks 
Step. The best cover save a unit can hope for is a 4+.  
 
It is ultimately up to the game master as to what constitutes granting 
cover save, and some examples are given below: 
▶ 6+: hedges, plasterboard, etc. 
▶ 5+: ruins, debris, car doors, etc. 
▶ 4+: stone walls, castle battlements, etc. 
 
 
 
Surprise 
Sometimes one side in combat has the element of surprise over 
another. It is also possible that both sides blunder into each other 
without warning. When this is the case, the members of the side that 
might be surprised (which could be both) should save vs INT. On a 
failure, they are surprised and can make no actions during the first 
round of combat. 
 
Disengages and Attacks of Opportunity 
When a unit is adjacent unit in combat, they can be assumed to be 
‘engaged’. This means that if an engaged unit leaves this adjacency, 
then the unit they are moving away from may make a free attack 
test with their melee weapons. This is called an attack of 
opportunity. A unit may declare it is using its action for a round to 
disengage, which means it may move away without provoking an 
attack of opportunity, but then it may not use its action to do 
anything else. 
 
Player Characters in Destroyed Units 
If a player character is in a destroyed unit, they must immediately 
save vs AGI or save vs END (player choice). A failure means that 
they have been badly injured, possibly knocked out and/or taken 
prisoner. If they fail this save, they must immediately save vs END 
again with a successive failure meaning that they have been killed 
in the fighting. If either save is passed, then the character survives 
the destruction of the unit. 
 
 
 
 
 
The War Engine 
The War Engine is a second set of mass combat rules that a game 
master can use when deciding how to mediate combats involving 
large volumes of troops. The War Engine differs from the Battle 
Engine in that is significantly reduces the player involvement and 
choice – the entire combat is handled by a single opposed dice roll.  
 
Force Value  
 
The force value is a value added to the combat test made to 
determine the outcome of a battle it. It calculated as follows: 
 
▶ 1. Calculate Force Value for Each Combatant: For each type of 
combatant in a force, multiply the number of them by their level.  
▶ 2. Add Each Combatant Value: Once you have completed step 
1, add all of the values together and then divide that total by 100. 
This will give you the base force value. 
▶ 3. Calculate Bonus Value: Take the base force value and 
divide it by 10. This will give you the bonus value. 
▶ 4. Add Bonus Value: For each of the following statements that 
are true, add the bonus value to the base force value. 
 
Add 1 x bonus value to the base force value for each of the 
following statements that are true. 
▶ Flying: The force contains aerial creatures, vehicles or units. 
▶ Cavalry Support: The force contains at least 10% cavalry units. 
▶ Cavalry Mainstay: The force contains at least 40% cavalry units. 
▶ Missile Support: The force contains at least 10% missile units. 
▶ Missile Mainstay: The force contains at least 40% missile units. 
▶ Missile Army: The force contains at least 80% missile units. 
▶ Monstrous Presence: The force contains at least one creature or 
vehicle of huge size or larger. 
▶ Monstrous Support: The force contains at least 30% creatures 
or vehicles of huge size or greater. 
 
Add 3 x bonus value to the base force value for each of the 
following statements that are true. 
▶ Entirely Flying: Every unit of the force has access to aerial 
vehicles or movement. 
▶ Cavalry Support: The force contains at least 10% armoured 
cavalry units. 
▶ Cavalry Mainstay: The force contains at least 40% cavalry units. 
▶ Artillery Support: At least 10% of the force contains 
creatures/vehicles whose main attack has the siege weapon quality 
(do not include the siege quality conferred on huge and larger 
creatures in this). 
▶ Line Artillery: At least 30% of the force contains 
creatures/vehicles whose main attack has the siege weapon quality 
(do not include the siege quality conferred on huge and larger 
creatures in this). 
 
▶ 5. Add Character Value: For each player character in the army 
add a bonus to the base force value that is equal to their level 
multiplied by their hit points. 
 
Example: Orc Army 
Below is an example of an army statblock created using orcs, ogres 
and goblins. 
 
▶ 10,000 orcs: Value: 200 
▶ 2,000 ogres: Value: 60 
▶ 20,000 goblins: Value: 100 
Base Force Value = 360 
 
Bonus Value = 36 
+ Missile Support + Missile Mainstay 
 
Total Force Value = 432 


Game Mastery 
122 
 
Resolving War Engine Battles 
To resolve a war engine battle, a game master should complete the 
following steps: 
 
1. Add Situational Modifiers 
The game master should add situational modifiers to each force’s, 
total force value as appropriate. Some examples are given below: 
 
▶ Heavily Fortified: If the army is heavily fortified, such as in a 
defending a castle or within a concrete bunker complex, then the 
game master should add 300% to the total force value. 
 
▶ Fighting Over Favourable Terrain: If the army is fighting over 
favourable terrain, such as hillmen defending tight mountain trails 
against outlander invaders, then the game master should add 50% 
to the total force value. 
 
▶ Surprise: If the army takes their foes by surprise, then the game 
master should add 100% to the total force value. 
 
2. Declare Battle Plan 
Each side declares its battle plan from one of five possible plans: 
▶ All-Out Defence: The army waits in place and does its best to 
take up tactical defensive positions. A force on the all-out defence 
will hang back and skirmish, rarely inflicting losses on their foe in 
order to minimise their own casualties. 
▶ Hold the Line: An army that is holding the line will fight 
defensively, not moving to engage the enemy but not giving 
quarter either. It will refuse to retreat and ensure it doesn’t give 
ground easily.  
▶ Measured Engagement: If 
an army engages the foe 
in a measured way, then 
they are carefully using 
their resources to 
maximise their attacks 
whilst minimising their 
own casualties. There is a 
real acceptance that losses 
must be taken to secure 
meaningful gains in the field. 
▶ Aggressive Advance: The army commanders through their 
decision making behind the concept of speed and aggression, 
hoping to overwhelm their foe with sheer hostility. 
▶ All-Out Assault: The commander of this army shows little 
regard for their own troops as they throw unit after unit into the 
fray with a simple order: destroy the enemy.  
 
3. Roll Battle Die 
Both side rolls 1d100 and adds their Total Force Value to the roll, 
This total is known as the side’s battle score. The side with the 
highest battle score is the victor. 
 
Overkill 
Before rolling the battle dice, divide the larger Total Force Value 
by the smaller Total Force Value. If the result is 5 or more, do not 
roll the battle dice. The larger force automatically wins with no 
casualties and losing force is annihilated. 
 
4. Apply the Result 
To work out the success of each side in the battle, consult the table 
below. Before you do this though, you must work out the base 
casualty rate (BCR). To do this divide the victor’s battle score by 
the loser’s battle score and multiply the result by 10. This is the 
BCR: a percentage referenced in the table below. The army suffers 
causalities equal to this percentage (which may be over 100%, 
meaning the destruction of the fighting force). 
 
What do casualties mean? 
We can assume that when part of a force is taken to be casualties 
that roughly half these combatants are killed outright, and the 
other half are injured in some way, taking them out of the fight 
(possibly as prisoners).  
 
Player Characters in War Engine Battles 
After a battle each player character must roll the appropriate BCR 
for their army’s results entry by using a d100. If the player rolls 
equal to or under the listed value (such as BCRx2) on the d100, 
then they have been taken as a casualty, suffering serious injury. If 
this is the case, they must immediately save vs END again with a 
failure meaning that they have been killed in the fighting.

123 
 
Designing a Campaign
Designing Foes 
Designing monstrous horrors for a campaign can be some of the 
most fun a game master can have. Below are some guidelines to help 
you determine generally balanced statistics and experience point 
values. It is emphasised that these are guidelines and not hard and 
fast rules or limits. There will be exceptions to all of the following. 
 
1. Concept 
Firstly, a game master must have a concept or idea that they wish to 
achieve in order to design an interesting foe. They should start by 
considering the relative power of the creature: a variant of tunnel-
building goblin may only level 1 or 2, but an antediluvian eldritch 
horror may be level 25+. Choosing the level of a creature is a good 
place to start. Remember, the level of a creature will determine its 
target numbers for skills as well as its hit points. 
 
2. Core Attributes and Skills 
The game master should decide what they want the proficiency level 
of the core skills of a creature to be. A point worth noting is that it 
is far more likely that a higher-level creature would have attributes 
and skills of Master and Grandmaster than a lower-level creature.
