5. Determine Actions and Abilities 
A game master should now decide on a creature’s actions and 
abilities. This should include any attacks that a creature can make. 
The bestiaries found within this book can help inspire a game master 
as to the sort of actions and abilities may be available to a creature.  
 
Damage 
The damage of creatures can be hard to decide. Helpfully, the 
following table can act as a guide for the amount of damage an attack 
by a specific creature may do, taking into account its size, level and 
the skill proficiency that it uses to make its attack test. 
 
6. Fill in the Numbers 
The game master should now make a judgement about the 
remaining qualities of their creature and fill them in such as the 
Number Appearing. 
 
7. Experience Points 
To calculate the value of a creature’s experience points, start by 
examining their hit points. Then add the bonus value for each power 
that they have. Powers are roughly defined as the following: 
▶ Significant Armour Saves: Any creature with an armour save of 
3+ or better. 
▶ Power Saves: Any creature with any form of power save. 
▶ Flying: Any creature that can fly, so long as within the setting this 
is a significant benefit. 
▶ Multiple Attacks: Any creature that can make multiple attacks per 
round. 
▶ Recharge Abilities: Any creature that has an ability that uses the 
recharge rules. 
▶ Unbreakable Morale: Any creature that has a morale rating of 1+. 
▶ Dramatic Actions: Any creature that has access to dramatic 
actions. 
▶ Special Attacks and Defences: Any creature that has access 
special attacks, defences, auras etc.

8. Dramatic and Mook Creatures 
Once a creature has been completed there is one additional way it 
may be upgraded further, and that is by making it a dramatic 
creature or a mook creature. Which are defined as the following: 
 
Dramatic Creatures 
Dramatic creatures are incredibly powerful, often creatures out of 
myth and legend or named, high-ranking ‘boss’ creatures. To make 
a dramatic creature, a game master should give the creature a 
number of dramatic actions that they can take out of sequence, 
such as attacks and movement. This increases the XP value of the 
creature by one bonus level.  
 
Mook Creatures 
Mooks are low ranking henchmen. For example, in the Heroic 
Fantasy setting an adult dragon may have several ogres as thralls to 
do its dirty work. Mechanically, mooks are designed by the game 
master to pose a challenge but are otherwise chaff to control a 
battlefield and to ensure a dramatic, engaging combat that doesn’t 
drag. A game master can designate any creature to be a mook. 
 
If a creature is a mook, the following rules apply: 
▶ If the creature suffers any unsaved damage, it immediately drops 
to 0HP, regardless of its actual total HP. 
▶ If the creature is forced to make a save to halve the damage of 
any affect and it succeeds, it suffers no damage.  
▶ Every other element of the creature works as it usually should: 
damage, actions, skills, etc. They are all unchanged. 
▶ A mook creature’s xp is reduced. To calculate the new experience 
point value, divide the creature’s usual experience point value by its 
usual HP. 
 


Designing a Campaign 
125 
 
Designing New Talent Trees 
Sooner or later, it is likely that any budding game master will 
desire to create their own talent trees for new classes. This is 
entirely possible (and encouraged) by the designer of Advancd 
Fantasy.  
 
Design Principles 
There are a few guiding and hitherto unexpressed principles that 
guided the construction of the talent trees in this book. They are as 
follows: 
 
Heritage Trees 
▶ Restrictions: All heritage trees require a character to have a 
particular heritage. 
▶ Number of Talents: All heritage trees contain 9 talents. 
▶ Other Guidance: The highest talent on each heritage tree usually 
requires a character to be 8th level. 
 
Class Trees 
▶ Restrictions: All classes are largely one of the following: mages, 
priests, rogues or warriors. While some classes allow characters to 
be between these different archetypes, most talent trees focus on 
one. 
▶ Number of Talents: All heritage trees contain 40 talents, which is 
more than a character can usually attain at level 30, meaning that a 
character will always have to choose how they specialise within their 
own class. 
 
Major and Minor Skills 
Some skills are intrinsically locked behind talents, to ensure that 
characters must dedicate time to specialising in them, and to 
ensure that they only access them in a timely manner and not too 
quickly. We call these skills Major Skills. Some skills however are 
the domain of many adventurers and are freely found in several 
skill pools. We call these skills Minor Skills. If a game master is 
designing a Talent Tree is important that they consider which 
major skills they want to offer, tying these to talents, and which 
minor skills they wish to be a part of the skill pools for that talent 
tree. 
 
MAJOR AND MINOR SKILLS 
Minor Skills 
Major Skills

The Threshold Talent 
All classes have a ‘threshold talent’ which marks a significant 
moment of commitment to that class. This talent is usually placed 
around a third of the way up the tree. The threshold talent always 
requires the character to have taken at least six talents in that tree 
and provides +2 FP. Examples of threshold talents include 
Vocational Discernment and Vows of Duty. 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
Healing and Resting 
A large part of the game is internally ‘balanced’ and organised 
around the adventuring day and how quickly, or slowly, a 
character can restore hit points and fatigue points. For this reason, 
it is important that any time a character may gain access to a talent 
or ability that grants access to healing, it should cost FP, or have a 
restricted usage per day, or both. One particular issue to pay 
attention to is the cost of spells. Some talents reduce the cost of 
spells, but this should never be used to reduce the cost of spells 
which provide healing to 0FP. 
 
Subsystems 
There are several subsystems around which the game is built. 
Placing opportunities to interact with these subsystems through 
talents can be very fun. A list of the subsystems is placed below, to 
remind a game master of what they are. 
 
▶ Alchemy: Some characters gain the ability to brew potions and 
tinctures. 
▶ Enchanting: Some characters gain the ability to craft magic items. 
▶ Magic and Spells: Some characters gain access to spells. 
Remember, in general terms a character may never gain access to 
more than one sphere of magic. 
▶ Mass Combat: Some characters gain talents that affect their 
abilities in a mass battle using the Battle Engine or War Engine rules. 
▶ Runes: Some characters gain the ability to craft runes, which can 
be applied to armour, weapons, stones and flesh. 
▶ Strongholds and Domains: Some characters gain talents that 
affect their control over any stronghold or domains under their rule.  
 
 
 


Designing a Campaign 
126 
 
Dramatic Concepts 
Dramatic concepts are particular rule modifications or additions that 
can be added to a campaign in order to provoke a particular 
atmosphere or style of play. 
 
Aging – Healing Magic 
Some magical spells, specifically Raise Dead and Restoration 
consume a number of years of a volunteer’s life. The number of years 
taken here (10 years for Raise Dead and 5 for Restoration) assume 
the player character is a human. With this dramatic concept, the 
number of years taken is changed to be a percentage of the 
character’s life, instead of the fixed number of years. This greatly 
increases the value of healing spells and their use in the mid-
campaign period.  
 
If this dramatic concept is used, then it is recommended that Raise 
Dead consumes 10% of the volunteer’s life, and Restoration 
consumes 5%. For example, if elves in your campaign live to be 2,000 
then if they volunteer to be the character to give up a portion of 
their life span to raise their dead human companion, then the casting 
will extract roughly 200 years’ worth of their life. 
 
Brutal Injuries 
The Brutal Injuries concept is a way of extending the damage system 
found in JEM at the cost of a little bit more book-keeping. If this 
dramatic concept is used, then when a creature drops to 0HP they 
are not knocked unconscious and dying. This replaces the standard 
rules for death and dying and critical hits. 
 
When a creature is knocked to 0HP, the attacker should calculate 
how much damage they did beyond knocking the creature to 0. This 
is the excess damage. For example, if a character with 2 current HP  
is hit by an attack that does 8 damage, then they suffer 6 excess 
damage. If the attack is a critical that doesn’t reduce a creature to 
below 0, then they should continue with an excess damage of 0. 
 
The attacker should then roll a d20 on the following table, and 
compare the result with the proficiency level of the skill they used 
for the attack test to determine a location of where their successful 
attack has just landed:

Gold as Experience 
Within this campaign, gold has a value beyond its mere worth as 
treasure. To encourage characters to delve deeper into forgotten 
crypts and haul out vast quantities of precious metals, in this 
campaign the game master should reward players with experience 
points in relation to the amount of treasure received, according to 
the following rules:  
 
▶ A character should be rewarded 1 experience point for each gold 
piece’s worth of treasure they recover from an adventure site.  
▶ All non-magical treasure, such as coins, jewellery; precious stones; 
works of art, etc. should be granted experience points in this way. 
▶ An item is counted as recovered once it is removed to the safety 
of a settlement. It is not enough to simply remove from a dungeon 
environment if that means it is still in the vast wilderness. 
 
Hindrances 
Hindrances are the opposite of Talents. They are a mechanised form 
of the negative qualities of a character. This is a significant dramatic 
concept which has the potential to drastically change the game being 
played. No character needs to take a hindrance, but for every 
hindrance a character takes, they may choose an additional talent. 
This is usually done during character creation. It is advised that the 
game master set the limit on hindrances at two: anymore and a 
character can become difficult to effectively roleplay. The game 
master should also curate the list of available hindrances, disallowing 
those that might disrupt the focus or tone of their campaign. Below 
are the descriptions of hindrances. 
Addiction 
The character has an addiction to some form of consumable, such as 
alcohol, cigarettes, magical residuum or strangeroot. They must 
partake in their addiction at least once a day or gain 2 drained 
conditions which can increase indefinitely after each extended rest. 
They must use one of their carrying capacity slots to carry the object 
of their addiction, such as bottles of ale or pouches of tobacco. 
Whenever they are offered easy access to the object of their 
addiction, they must make a save vs PER or immediately begin to 
partake, regardless of the consequences. 
 
Armless/Handless 
The character is missing a hand or arm. This hindrance must be 
permanent, and therefore not easily treated with advanced 
technologies or magic. 
 
Avarice 
A limitless greed hangs over the character, which only subsides 
momentarily when they are given financial rewards. Whenever the 
character is given the opportunity to gain money, they must save vs 
PER. On a failure, they must immediately attempt to get the payoff, 
even if it is immoral, criminal or dangerous. 
 
Blindness 
The character is permanently blind and suffers a -6 to all tests that 
rely on sight. The blindness must be permanent, and therefore not 
easily treated with advanced technologies or magic. 
 
Bloodlust 
A character has a particular innate desire to hurt the creatures they 
dislike. This is a particularly evil hindrance and should be considered 
carefully by players and game masters. Whenever a creature attempts 
to parley with this character during a fight, or attempts to surrender, 
the character must save vs PER or attempt to kill their charges. In 
addition, this save must be repeated whenever they are put in charge 
or prisoners or given power over individuals they dislike. 
 
Coward 
The character is a coward – they avoid any situation that involves 
mortal peril or the chance of serious injury. Unfortunately, this 
includes combat, an aspect of most campaigns of JEM. Whenever a 
character finds themselves in such a situation, they must save vs 
PER or try to extricate themselves from the situation by the fastest 
possible means, even if this means an ignominious flight. They may 
repeat this save each round, decreasing its difficulty level by one 
each round after the first. After 1 turn (10 minutes) the test is 
considered passed automatically. 
 
Deafness 
The character is deaf. This only applies to heritages that normally 
have clear auditory function, such as humans. The character cannot 
hear anything at all. The deafness must be permanent, and therefore 
not easily treated with advanced technologies or magic. The 
character takes a -3 to all tests that would normally be made easier 
through being able to hear, such as attack tests in melee. 
 
Extra Rest 
The character needs more rest than others for some reason. Short 
rests for this character take two hours, extended rests take 12. 
 
Extreme Age 
This character is very old. In human terms, they are over the age of 
75. To take this hindrance, the character’s age must actually be a 
limitation (a 250-year-old elf who is as youthful as a 25 year old 
human should not take this hindrance) If a character has this 
hindrance, then their speed is halved and STR, AGI and END may 
not be increased above Trained. 
 
Fragile Integrity 
Due to some unknown reason, this character’s metabolism or 
internal structure is weaker than the average for their heritage. 
Whenever they drop to 0 hit points, instead of rolling a 1d4 to see 
how long they have until they die, assume the roll was a 1. In 
addition, if the character every takes damage from a single attack 
that is greater than half of their maximum hit points, then they must 
immediately make a hard (-3) save vs END or drop to 0 hit points. 
 
 


Designing a Campaign 
128 
 
Generous 
This character is kind and wants to help others no matter what. This 
puts them in the difficult position of sometimes helping those who 
are working against them or being used by others for their support. 
Whenever the character is ever asked for something, such as 
financial support, help with a task or any other such request for aid, 
then they must save vs PER with a failure indicating that they must 
help the requester, regardless of the consequence to themselves. 
 
Grotesquely Ugly 
This character is so ugly that they evoke revulsion or even fear in 
other creatures. This character suffers a -5 penalty to all reaction 
rolls. 
 
Insatiable Hunger  
This character enjoys food, a little too much. Food is one of the 
greatest joys and they refuse to go without. This character may not 
leave the safety of a settlement or home base without burdening 
themselves with at least three carrying capacity slots worth of food. 
The character consumes at least two meals during each extended 
rest and whenever they are offered food or drink, they must save vs 
PER or consume what they are being offered. 
 
Insomniac 
The character finds it very difficult to sleep. After each extended rest, 
roll 1d4. On 1, the character only has half of the maximum FP and 
they have 2 drained conditions. 
 
Lechery 
This character has an unreasonable need to physical intimacy with 
others. Whenever they find themselves in the presence of an NPC 
they find even vaguely attractive, they must save vs PER. On a 
failure, they must bring all of their charms to bear as best they can, 
regardless of the consequences. 
 
Miserliness 
This character hates spending money, for any reason and at any time. 
They will resist spending money even if it is to their own detriment. 
Whenever a character is required to spend their own money, they 
must save vs PER. A failure means they refuse to do so under any 
circumstances. If it is a requirement that they do so, then the 
character rages and fumes, haggling the whole while and then 
complaining endlessly about it afterwards. 
 
Missing Eye 
The character only has one functional eye. They take a -2 penalty to 
all tests that require the use of their sight. This hindrance must be 
permanent, and therefore not easily treated with advanced 
technologies or magic. 
 
Mute 
The character is unable to speak due to some form of physical, 
psychological, magical, or technological impairment. This is 
permanent and therefore not easily treated with advanced 
technologies or magic. 
 
Pacifist: Self Defence 
The character does not believe in harming other creatures. They may 
not attack any creature that has not physically tried to harm them 
first. 
 
Pacifist: Total Objection 
The character does not believe in violence. This is without exception. 
The character may not use any violent means against any other 
creature, regardless of any circumstances. This is a particularly 
challenging hindrance and therefore is usually worth two talents. 
 
Repellent to Animals 
Wild beasts do not like this character and even tame ones do their 
best to avoid them. Dogs bark aggressively, horses refuse to be 
ridden and cats hiss as they pass. On reaction rolls, animals suffer a 
-5 penalty to this character. 
 
Terrible Liar 
The character finds it incredibly hard to lie, oftentimes to their 
detriment. Whenever the character is lying to an NPC, the NPC may 
save vs INT. On a success, they know they are being lied to. 
Hit Locations in Combat 
Sometimes where a creature has struck another creature with an 
attack is important. For most campaigns, this will only be relevant 
for things such as critical hits, or for called shots. However, it may 
that a game master is designing a campaign where armour protects 
a character differently across the body or that they desire a greater 
granularity to their combat. This dramatic concept allows a game 
master to regularly include a hit location as part of the attack test. 
 
If this dramatic concept is used, then whenever an attack test is 
made, a d12 should also be rolled. The d12 corresponds to the table 
below, which will become familiar to players quickly in such a 
campaign and shows where the attack, if it hits, has been successful. 
 
HIT LOCATIONS TABLE 
D12 
Result 
1 
Left foot or leg (Game Master choice) 
2 
Right Foot or leg (Game Master choice) 
3-9 
Body 
10 
Offhand/arm 
11 
Main hand/arm 
12 
Head, Soft underbelly or other significantly vulnerable 
location (Game Master choice) 
 
Individual Initiative 
In this campaign, the game master may wish to resolve actions each 
round in a different way to the standard manner described in the 
core rules. Instead of each side working through the standard 
resolution and initiative steps, if this dramatic concept is used then 
each combatant completes their actions in their own specially-
designated step. A character need not move before taking any other 
actions and may instead take their action then move – all other rules 
apply.  
 
To determine the order of these individual imitative turns, the game 
master may wish each combatant to roll a d20, and work down the 
characters starting with the one who rolled the highest. 
 
In the case of large groups of monsters, the game master should give 
them a single initiative turn, resolving all their actions as a group 
instead of rolling individually. 
 
Frantic Initiative 
This is a sub-dramatic concept for the Individual Initiative concept. 
If this concept is used, then the following rules apply: each 
combatant should be assigned a unique token at the start of the 
combat. Each token should be placed in an opaque container such 
as a cup or bag. A token should be drawn out at random, and the 
character, or characters, that are assigned to this token activates and 
takes their turn. This is repeated until all tokens have been removed 
from the vessel, which point the round ends and all the tokens are 
returned to the container for the new round. 
 
This 
sub-dramatic 
concept 
is 
used 
to 
inject 
a 
frenzied 
unpredictability to combat and can be used to create suspense and 
tension to every single activation in combat. 
 
Opposed Attack Tests 
In a normal game of Advanced Fantasy it is assumed that when 
making an attack test, that it is a simple test carried out by the 
character. The game master might feel that such tests should be 
opposed as a rule, rather than by exception. If the game master elects 
to use this dramatic concept then attack skills like Melee should be 
made as opposed tests in combat. This requires more book-keeping 
on the game master’s behalf, and therefore it is a dramatic concept. 
Game masters who use this dramatic concept will find the target 
numbers at lower experience levels are generally lower, resulting in 
more hits from both sides. 
 
Damage Every Attack 
This is a sub-dramatic concept for the Opposed Attack Tests concept. 
If this dramatic concept is applied, then attack tests that are opposed 
with an attack skill, such as Melee vs Melee, then if the attacker fails 
their test, then it is the defender who gets to cause damage against 
them. With this dramatic concept, the amount of damage each round 
goes up significantly, and this can result in very fast, very bloody 
combats. 
 


 
129 
 
Grindhouse Mode
Grindhouse Mode is a style of gameplay used for creating characters. 
In a Grindhouse game, all players begin a scenario with four level 0 
characters. As a Grindhouse scenario progresses, each player works 
to maintain the survival for as many of their four characters as 
possible. 
 
This tricky endeavour is made more difficult by the fact that level 0 
characters only have 1 hit point and few other resources. Players 
must work together carefully to try and ensure that at least one 
character of each player survives the scenario. 
 
Each character in Grindhouse Mode has the following qualities: 
 
Random Heritage 
Each character created for a Grindhouse game has a random 
Heritage, as rolled by the table in the Heritage section of this book. 
 
Random Origin 
Each character has a random Origin, rolled on the table below. This 
determines what a character did before choosing to be an 
adventurer. It grants a character their starting equipment, as well as 
sometimes providing skills and possibly a talent. 
▶ Origin Talents: While some characters will have an Origin talent, 
this talent, like a heritage talent, is not considered a character’s ‘first 
talent’ that they would choose if they survived the Grindhouse 
scenario and achieve first level. 
 
 
Hit Points, Fatigue Points and Target Numbers 
▶ Hit Points: A level 0 character in a Grindhouse game has only a 
single HP and dies if they are reduced to 0 (they are not 
considered dying but are totally dead). 
▶ Fatigue Points: A level 0 character in a Grindhouse game has 
4FP which are pooled centrally. Of a player’s four characters, any 
of them can spend FP from the central pool at any time. Therefore, 
a player with four characters at the start of a Grindhouse game has 
a pool of 16FP. This total remains even if a character is killed. If 
the characters have a rest, then all of their FP returns to 16FP, even 
if there are fewer than four characters still alive. 
▶ Target Numbers: A level 0 character has the target numbers of 
the Jack Experience Path. If they become level 1, they may switch 
to Specialist if their player so wishes. 
 
Origin Equipment 
Each Origin begins with a random piece of equipment that the 
character should add to their sheet. For the sake of expedience, do 
not track carrying capacity slots in a Grindhouse game. 
 
Surviving a Grindhouse Scenario 
A Grindhouse scenario should only be used right at the start of a 
campaign to generate a group of adventurers and is generally only 
one or two sessions long. At the end of Grindhouse scenario, a 
player should survey their surviving characters and choose one to 
become level 1, choosing their first two talents and updating their 
HP and FP. They begin with 100xp already.

Origin Descriptions 
 
Animal Trainer 
Animal trainers interact with their charges, charming snakes and 
making badgers dance on their hind legs. 
▶ Origin Skills: +2 proficiency levels in Animal Handling. 
▶ Origin Equipment: A random animal, roll 1d6: (1) a badger; (2) 
a chicken; (3) a frog; (4) a monkey; (5) a poisonous snake; (6) a 
bear. 
▶ Origin Talent: None. 
 
Artisan 
Artisans are craftsmen who create sculptures, paintings, poetry and 
other works of art. 
▶ Origin Skills: +1 proficiency level in Craft (Painting) or Craft 
(Poetry) or Craft (Sculpture) and +1 proficiency level in Sleight 
of Hand. 
▶ Origin Equipment: The character gains a set of paint brushes if 
they are a painter, a chapbook of poetry if they are a poet, or a set 
of chisels and hammer if they are a sculptor. 
▶ Origin Talent: None. 
 
Baker 
Bakers work flour into dough, cooking and preparing bread. 
▶ Origin Skills: +1 proficiency level in Craft (Cookery) and +1 
proficiency level in Gossip. 
▶ Origin Equipment: A 3’ long baguette. 
▶ Origin Talent: None. 
 
 
 
 
Beggar 
Beggars are those so poor that they live on the streets, asking 
those who pass by to spare a coin or two. 
▶ Origin Skills: +1 proficiency level in Persuasion and +1 
proficiency level in Gossip. 
▶ Origin Equipment: A wooden bowl. 
▶ Origin Talent: None. 
 
Brewer 
Brewers craft beers and ales, often guarding the secret of their 
methods closely. 
▶ Origin Skills: +1 proficiency level in Craft (Ale) and +1 
proficiency level in Consume Alcohol. 
▶ Origin Equipment: A keg of strong ale. 
▶ Origin Talent: None.  
 
Carpenter 
Carpenters work with wood, turning putting it to a variety of uses 
such as wagon wheels and furniture. 
▶ Origin Skills: +1 proficiency level in Craft (Carpentry) and +1 
proficiency level in Search. 
▶ Origin Equipment: A saw and a hammer. 
▶ Origin Talent: None. 
 


Grindhouse Mode 
131 
 
Charcoal Burner 
Operating careful kilns, charcoal burners produce fuel ready to 
burn in homes and industry. 
▶ Origin Skills: +1 proficiency level in Survival. 
▶ Origin Equipment: A bag of charcoal. 
▶ Origin Talent: 
Soot-Born 
PASSIVE 
▶ The character has resistance to fire damage, meaning they halve 
all incoming fire damage. 
 
Circus Performer 
Members of travelling troupes, circus performers have a variety of 
skills.  
▶ Origin Skills: +1 proficiency level in Perform (Any) and +1 
proficiency level in Climb. 
▶ Origin Equipment: A garish outfit covered in bells and tassels. 
▶ Origin Talent: None. 
 
Cutpurse 
Petty thieves, pickpockets and cutpurses eke out a few coins per 
day by stealing them in busy thoroughfares. 
▶ Origin Skills: +1 proficiency level in Sleight of Hand and +1 
proficiency level in Stealth. 
▶ Origin Equipment: 1d6gp. 
▶ Origin Talent: None. 
 
 
Drunkard 
Some people find truth and meaning at the bottom of a bottle. But 
most find their ruin. 
▶ Origin Skills: +2 proficiency levels in Consume Alcohol. 
▶ Origin Equipment: A bottle of beer. 
▶ Origin Talent:  
Cup-Wise 
PASSIVE 
▶ While the character is over their drunk limit, they gain a 6+ 
power save and a +6 bonus to PER and INT saves. 
 
Engineer’s Apprentice 
Apprentice engineers work with masters in order to understand 
machinery, siegecraft and scientific principles. 
▶ Origin Skills: +1 proficiency level in Knowledge (Engineering) 
and +1 proficiency level in Set/Remove Traps. 
▶ Origin Equipment: 1d6 cherry bombs (see Magic Item 
Descriptions) 
▶ Origin Talent: None. 
 
Farmer 
Farmers work the land, raising animals and tending crops 
▶ Origin Skills: +1 proficiency level in Animal Handling and +1 
proficiency level in Survival. 
▶ Origin Equipment: A random farm good, roll 1d6: (1) 2d6 ears 
of corn; (2) a chicken; (3) a cow; (4) a dog; (5) 3d6 turnips; (6) a 
sickle. 
▶ Origin Talent: None. 
 


Grindhouse Mode

Ferryman 
Ferrymen operate ferries that cross rivers and lakes. 
▶ Origin Skills: +1 proficiency level in Knowledge (Sailing) and 
+1 proficiency level in Gossip. 
▶ Origin Equipment: A 10’ long barge pole. 
▶ Origin Talent: None. 
 
Fisherman 
Fisherman operate nets on trawlers, harvesting fish from oceans. 
▶ Origin Skills: +1 proficiency level in Knowledge (Sailing) and 
+1 proficiency level in Survival. 
▶ Origin Equipment: A random item, roll 1d6: (1-2) a net;  
(3-4) 50’ rope; (5-6) a suspiciously large cod. 
▶ Origin Talent: None. 
 
Friar/Nun 
Friars and nuns and other low-ranking clergy and members of the

▶ Origin Skills: +1 proficiency level in Knowledge (Religion) 
and +1 proficiency level in Pray. 
▶ Origin Equipment: 6 incense cones. 
▶ Origin Talent: None. 
 
Gladiator 
Fighters for sports and money, gladiators know how to make a 
spectacle of violence. 
▶ Origin Skills: +1 proficiency level in Melee and +1 proficiency 
level in Perform (Stage Combat). 
▶ Origin Equipment: A spear. 
▶ Origin Talent: None. 
 
Gravedigger 
Gravediggers prepare plots of land for the internment of the dead. 
▶ Origin Skills: +1 proficiency level in Climb and +1 proficiency 
level in Knowledge (Religion). 
▶ Origin Equipment: A shovel. 
▶ Origin Talent: None. 
 
Hedge Witch 
Hedge witches are a type of cunning folk that work as the centre 
of rural communities. 
▶ Origin Skills: +1 proficiency level in Spellcasting. The 
character also learns one 1st spell from the primal sphere which 
they can now cast. 
▶ Origin Equipment: A piece of heather or another non-magical 
gewgaw. 
▶ Origin Talent: None. 
 
Hunter 
Hunters track down animals for their meat, leather and other useful 
resources. 
▶ Origin Skills: +1 proficiency level in Ranged and +1 proficiency 
level in Tracking. 
▶ Origin Equipment: A short bow. 
▶ Origin Talent: None. 
 
Jester 
Hilarious clowns, jesters are often the only people in a ruler’s court 
that can speak truth to power. 
▶ Origin Skills: +1 proficiency level in Perform (Dance) and +1 
proficiency level in Perform (Jokes). 
▶ Origin Equipment: A silly hat. 
▶ Origin Talent: None. 
 
Mercenary 
Hired swords can be found in the army of every petty lord and 
warlord’s entourage. 
▶ Origin Skills: +1 proficiency level in Melee and +1 proficiency 
level in Knowledge (Tactics). 
▶ Origin Equipment: A short sword. 
▶ Origin Talent: None. 
Merchant 
Masters of mercantile, merchants work in the acquisition of wealth 
through business. 
▶ Origin Skills: +1 proficiency level in Haggle and +1 proficiency 
level in Survival. 
▶ Origin Equipment: A random trade good, roll 1d6: (1) a bottle 
find wine worth 150gp; (2) a bag of rare spices worth 250gp; (3) a 
block of salt worth 40gp; (4) a long sword; (5) a rack of eels worth 
10gp; (6) a gem worth 600gp. 
▶ Origin Talent: None. 
 
Miner 
Miners labour beneath the world, picking and hacking out coal, 
iron and other more precious things. 
▶ Origin Skills: +1 proficiency level in END. 
▶ Origin Equipment: A pickaxe. 
▶ Origin Talent: None. 
 
Mutant 
Whether through unnatural lineage or exposure to corrupting 
magics, sometimes humanoids are born with weird mutations. 
▶ Origin Skills: +1 proficiency level in Melee and +1 proficiency 
level in Survival. 
▶ Origin Equipment: None 
▶ Origin Talent: A random mutation, roll 1d8: (1) gills; (2) 
ironskin; (3) feathers; (4) a third eye; (5) a ghastly stench; (6) a 
prehensile tail; (7) a poisonous bite; (8) eyes in the back of your 
head. 
 


Grindhouse Mode 
133 
 
Peasant 
The fields are worked by peasants, semi-indentured workers that 
owe fealty to their lords. 
▶ Origin Skills: +1 proficiency level in STR or END (player 
choice). 
▶ Origin Equipment: A hoe. 
▶ Origin Talent: None. 
 
Pirate 
Pirates are malicious sailors who survive by attacking and robbing 
other ships, often feeding their crews to the sharks. 
▶ Origin Skills: +1 proficiency level in Knowledge (Sailing) and 
+1 proficiency level in Intimidate. 
▶ Origin Equipment: A cutlass (count as a short sword). 
▶ Origin Talent: None. 
 
Rune Keeper 
Low-ranking rune smiths are often given a glimpse of the 
geomantic knowledge that they could earn. 
▶ Origin Skills: +1 proficiency level in Craft (Runes). The 
character also learns one lesser rune which they can now trace. 
▶ Origin Equipment: A chisel and hammer. 
▶ Origin Talent: None. 
 
Sailor 
Working on ships and travelling the many oceans of the world, 
sailors are often more comfortable at sea that on land. 
▶ Origin Skills: +1 proficiency level in Knowledge (Sailing) and 
+1 proficiency level in Melee. 
▶ Origin Equipment: A cutlass (count as a short sword). 
▶ Origin Talent: None. 
 
Slave 
Slaves are taken by wicked humanoids, often living a life of abject 
misery. Some are fortunate to escape such a life. 
▶ Origin Skills: +1 proficiency level in Forage/Hunting and +1 
proficiency level in Survival. 
▶ Origin Equipment: A set of manacles. 
▶ Origin Talent: None. 
 
Soldier 
Soldiers make up the professional standing armies of kings and 
dukes. 
▶ Origin Skills: +1 proficiency level in Armour and +1 proficiency 
level in Melee. 
▶ Origin Equipment: A short sword and leather armour. 
▶ Origin Talent: None. 
 
Squire 
Squires are the servants of knights, preparing their armour and 
weapons with the hopes of one day becoming a knight themselves. 
▶ Origin Skills: +1 proficiency level in Melee and +1 proficiency 
level in Shield. 
▶ Origin Equipment: A short sword and heater shield. 
▶ Origin Talent: None. 
 
Stevedore 
Stevedores are hardy dock workers who load and unload the hulls 
of ships. 
▶ Origin Skills: +1 proficiency level in STR. 
▶ Origin Equipment: A 50’ of rope. 
▶ Origin Talent: None. 
 
Student 
Students may be taught by a specific mentor in their village tower 
or be an attendant to a large university or other institution. 
▶ Origin Skills: +1 proficiency level in Consume Alcohol and +1 
proficiency level in Knowledge (Any). 
▶ Origin Equipment: A blank book, quill pen and ink. 
▶ Origin Talent: None. 
 
Tomb Robber 
Some criminals make their money by plundering tombs and 
burgling mausolea. 
▶ Origin Skills: +1 proficiency level in Force Doors and +1 
proficiency level in Streetwise. 
▶ Origin Equipment: A lantern and pint of oil. 
▶ Origin Talent: None. 
 
Trapper 
Trappers are those hunters specialising in snares and other forms 
of capture to hunt their prey. 
▶ Origin Skills: +1 proficiency level in Knowledge (Nature) and 
+1 proficiency level in Set/Remove Traps. 
▶ Origin Equipment: A bear trap. 
▶ Origin Talent: None. 
 
Urchin 
Urchins eke out a thin existence on the streets of large cities, 
finding their next meal any way they can. 
▶ Origin Skills: +1 proficiency level in Sleight of Hand and +1 
proficiency level in Streetwise. 
▶ Origin Equipment: 1d6cp. 
▶ Origin Talent: None. 
 
Weaver 
Weavers take cotton and wool and spin it into textiles, often in the 
cottages in which they also live. 
▶ Origin Skills: +1 proficiency level in Craft (Clothing) and +1 
proficiency level in Haggle. 
▶ Origin Equipment: A sheet of cloth that measures 8’ by 12’. 
▶ Origin Talent: None. 
 
Wizard’s Apprentice 
Apprentice wizards work with masters of the arcane arts hoping to 
one day develop their own magical powers. 
▶ Origin Skills: +1 proficiency level in Spellcasting. The 
character also learns one 1st spell from the arcane sphere which 
they can now cast. 
▶ Origin Equipment: A random 1st level scroll from the arcane 
sphere (roll on Appendix Table 1B). 
▶ Origin Talent: None. 
 
Woodcutter 
Woodcutters work in forests and woodland, felling trees. 
▶ Origin Skills: +1 proficiency level in Knowledge (Nature) and 
+1 proficiency level in Melee. 
▶ Origin Equipment: An axe. 
▶ Origin Talent: None. 


 
134 
 
Treasure
Generating Treasure 
Treasure is collected by all manner of nefarious creatures across the 
cosmos: dragons covet wealth, liches prize magical lore and 
occulators wish to collect anything that may be used against them in 
future.  
 
Individual vs Lair Treasure 
There are two main types of treasure in the game – individual and 
lair. 
Individual Treasure 
Individual treasure refers to that carried by single monsters that they 
could reasonably have during a fight – the orc carrying a couple 
silver pieces for example. It’s good to roll on these treasure tables if 
the party want to loot fallen enemies after a random encounter. 
 
Lair Treasure 
Lair treasure refers to those valuables that have been gathered into 
a great treasury or hoard by a powerful monster such as occulator, 
or a whole tribe or orcs or gnolls. An adventure site will typically 
have only location with lair treasure. 
 
Individual Treasure Table 
When a single creature is looted, roll 1d100 and consult the table below. Add the level of the creature as a modifier to the roll:

Crafting Magic Items 
As player characters grow in competency and power, they will 
naturally start to desire to make their own magic items, if only to 
replicate some of the ancient artefacts they have found within the 
world of your campaign. 
 
Magical items can be made with either a Craft (Alchemy) or Craft 
(Magic Items) skill test after spending the appropriate amount of 
money on components and time on the crafting process as outlined 
in a magic item’s description. 
 
Magical Formulae 
All magical items, from potions to swords, must be crafted using a 
formula. A character does not automatically know how to create a 
formula for a magical item. All the formulae a character knows must 
be stored in a formula book. 
 
Learning Magical Formulae and Adding them to a Formula Book 
To learn a magical formula, a character must first possess the item 
they wish to create. They may dismantle the object with a chance of 
learning its secrets and copying the formula into a formula book. 
The successful chance of learning and copying this formula is based 
on the INT proficiency level of the character, as seen on the table 
below: 
 
CHANCE OF COPYING FORMULA 
INT 
% Chance 
Untrained 
60% 
Trained 
85% 
Journeyman 
90% 
Expert 
95% 
Master 
98% 
Grandmaster 
99% 
   
Magical Armour and Weapons 
Magical armour and weapons are not crafted in the same way as 
other magical items. A character must still have a formula, but it is 
not for specific items. Instead, when a character destroys a magical 
weapon or piece of armour to learn its formula, they instead roll to 
copy the formula for the following: 
▶ Attack Bonus: Any magical bonus a weapon granted to attack 
tests. This may be to all attack tests or to specific types of enemies, 
such as undead or dragons. 
▶ Damage Bonus: Any magical bonus a weapon granted to damage. 
▶ Rend: Any magical bonus a weapon granted to rend value. 
▶ Talents: Any talents that were held by the weapon or armour 
(only weapon talents may be applied to weapons and only armour 
talents may be applied to armour). 
▶ Power Save: Any power save the item grants. 
 
 


Treasure 
136 
 
Crafting Magical Weapons and Armour 
To craft a magic weapon or piece of magic armour, a creature must 
have a mundane, non-magical version of that item that they are 
going to enchant. To enchant the item, they must pay the cost of all 
the formulae being applied. The time taken to create a magical 
armour or weapon is 1 day x 1000gp of the total cost combined. The 
value of a piece of enchanted armour or weapon is double the cost 
of all the formulae combined. 
 
Formulae of Different Tiers 
Some magical items have different tiers. When a copy of that item is 
destroyed and its formula learned, a character learns the formula for 
all tiers of that item.

To generate magic armour, start by rolling the armour type, then the

*This table is only rolled for predominately metal armour like 
chainmail and plate, not for shields or leather, etc. 
 
Mithril Armour 
If a piece of magical armour is made of mithril, then it makes the 
wearer immune to critical hits while it is worn. A character with the 
Craft (Armour) skill can make mithril armour at the base cost of the 
armour x 20, provided their proficiency level is Master. In technical 
terms, mithril is not magical and does not count as such, it is 
included here due to its exceptional rarity. 
 
Adamantine Armour 
If a piece of magical armour is made of adamantine, then it makes 
the wearer immune to critical hits and ignores all Rend from 
incoming attacks while it is worn. A character with the Craft 
(Armour) skill can make adamantine armour at the base cost of the 
armour x 100, provided their proficiency level is Grandmaster. In 
technical terms, adamantine is not magical and does not count as 
such, it is included here due to its exceptional rarity.

A creature wearing this armour counts as having a fly speed equal 
to their walk speed.

A creature wearing this armour counts as having a swim speed equal 
to their walk speed. In addition, the creature can breathe water as 
easily as air. 
▶ Component Cost: 5,200gp. 
▶ Requires Expert Craft (Magic Items) to add this talent to a piece 
of armour.

An animal wishing to attack the wearer of this armour must make a

piece of armour.

▶ Component Cost: 7,900gp.

Clear-Thought Fabric 
While wearing this armour, the wearer removes 3 stunned 
conditions per round instead of 1.  
▶ Component Cost: 900gp. 
▶ Requires Trained Craft (Magic Items) to add this talent to a piece

Cluckle-Leap Armour 
A creature wearing this armour counts as having a fly speed equal 
to their walk speed. However, they must not end their move flying, 
they must be in contact with a solid surface that they can stand on. 
▶ Component Cost: 3,800gp. 
▶ Requires Journeyman Craft (Magic Items) to add this talent to a 
piece of armour. 
 
 
 
Contortionist Sigils 
While wearing this armour, a character can contort themselves into 
impossible sizes and even compress their own flesh and bones. 
While contorted in this way, a character suffers a -9 penalty to all 
tests involving movement. However, they can squeeze themselves 
into any space 8” wide or more.  
▶ Component Cost: 1,200gp. 
▶ Requires Trained Craft (Magic Items) to add this talent to a piece 
of armour. 
 
Deathleap Weave 
When the wearer of this armour reduces an enemy to 0HP they may 
spend 1FP to immediately teleport up to 30’. 
▶ Component Cost: 3,300gp. 
▶ Requires Journeyman Craft (Magic Items) to add this talent to a 
piece of armour. 
 
Displacement Patterns 
While wearing this armour, the wearer may spend 2FP at the start 
of the round to activate the displacement patterns. These patterns 
last until the end of the round. While activated, all attack tests made 
against the wearer suffer a -3 penalty. 
▶ Component Cost: 5,000gp. 
▶ Requires Expert Craft (Magic Items) to add this talent to a piece 
of armour. 
 
Dwarfen Tracery 
A creature wearing this armour may reroll any failed save vs STR or 
save vs END. 
▶ Component Cost: 3,400gp. 
▶ Requires Journeyman Craft (Magic Items) to add this talent to a 
piece of armour.

Magic Weapons with Personality 
If a weapon has a PER proficiency level, then it is considered 
sentient. It gains an alignment (roll below on F3) and can 
communicate with its bearer telepathically. If they wielder of the 
weapon ever attempts an action that goes against the weapon’s 
desire, alignment or purpose, it may provoke an opposed PER test 
and if the weapon wins, it will prevent the wielder from carrying out 
the intended action. 
 
While wielding a weapon with a PER proficiency level, the wielder 
may use the weapon’s PER instead of their own so long as they 
remain carrying it. 
If a character is crafting a magic weapon as opposed to it being 
found in the game world, then they may imbue it with a personality, 
providing they can pay the component cost, and have a copy of the 
formula to hand in a formula book. If they do this, they may choose 
the weapon’s alignment.

1-33

34-66

Bloodletter 
The wielder may spend 2FP after successfully hitting any enemy to 
cause 1 bleeding condition. 
▶ Component Cost: 8,000gp. 
▶ Requires Journeyman Craft (Magic Items) to add to a weapon. 
 
Chaos Tempering 
While carrying this weapon, a character’s maximum hit points are 
reduced by 2 per tier. Additionally, the weapon deals an additional 
2 damage per tier. 
▶ Component Cost: 12,000gp per tier. 
▶ Requires Expert Craft (Magic Items) to add tier 1 to a weapon. 
▶ Requires Grandmaster Craft (Magic Items) to add tier 2 to a 
weapon. 
 
Commanding 
▶ Tier 1: While holding this weapon, the number of retainers a 
character can have based on their PER proficiency level is multiplied 
by 5. 
▶ Tier 2: While holding this weapon, the number of retainers a 
character can have based on their PER proficiency level is multiplied 
by 25. 
 
▶ Component Cost: 8,000gp (tier 1); 19,000gp (tier 2). 
▶ Requires Journeyman Craft (Magic Items) to add tier 1 to a 
weapon. 
▶ Requires Master Craft (Magic Items) to add tier 2 to a weapon.

The wielder may choose to change the damage caused by the 
weapon to acid damage. They may also spend 2FP after successfully 
hitting any enemy to cause an additional 1 x tier acid damage. 
▶ Component Cost: 4,000gp per tier. 
▶ Requires Trained Craft (Magic Items) to add tier 1 to a weapon. 
▶ Requires Expert Craft (Magic Items) to add tier 2 to a weapon. 
▶ Requires Grandmaster Craft (Magic Items) to add tier 3 to a 
weapon. 
 
Counterspelling 
While carrying this weapon, a character may attempt to counterspell 
one additional spell per round than usual. 
▶ Component Cost: 18,000gp per tier. 
▶ Requires Master Craft (Magic Items) to add to a weapon. 
 
Flaming 
The wielder may choose to change the damage caused by the 
weapon to fire damage. They may also spend 2FP after successfully 
hitting any enemy to cause an additional 1 x tier fire damage.

▶ Requires Trained Craft (Magic Items) to add tier 1 to a weapon. 
▶ Requires Expert Craft (Magic Items) to add tier 2 to a weapon. 
▶ Requires Grandmaster Craft (Magic Items) to add tier 3 to a 
weapon. 
 
Flickering Step

may also spend 2FP to disengage from an enemy without using an 
action. 
▶ Component Cost: 6,000gp. 
▶ Requires Journeyman Craft (Magic Items) to add to a weapon. 
 
Freezing 
The wielder may choose to change the damage caused by the 
weapon to cold damage. They may also spend 2FP after successfully 
hitting any enemy to cause an additional 1 x tier cold damage.

▶ Requires Trained Craft (Magic Items) to add tier 1 to a weapon.

Lightning 
The wielder may choose to change the damage caused by the 
weapon to lightning damage. They may also spend 2FP after 
successfully hitting any enemy to cause an additional 1 x tier 
lightning damage.

Sharpness

Vorpal Weapon 
Creatures of 10th level or lower that are critically hit by this weapon 
are automatically beheaded and killed. If the creature is of 11th level 
or higher, or their neck cannot physically be severed in a single 
blow, or they do not have a head to remove, then they suffer an 
additional 8 damage and gain 4 bleeding conditions. 
▶ Component Cost: 25,000gp. 
▶ Requires Grandmaster Craft (Magic Items) to add to a weapon.

Weapon of Spell-Storing 
This magic weapon contains a spell. While wielding this weapon the 
caster can ignore ‘forgotten’ entries on casting tables for the spell 
stored in the weapon. Additionally, the stored spell costs 1FP fewer 
to cast while the weapon is held (to a minimum of 0FP). In addition, 
the character must still have the components normally required for 
the spell. A character may attempt to cast the spell contained within 
the weapon regardless of their own spellcasting proficiency level. 
 
If this weapon is generated as part of a hoard, roll below to see what 
level of spell is contained in the weapon. Then roll on the 
appropriate random spell table in Appendix 1.

A creature that is confused may save vs PER at the end of each 
round to end the confusion. While confused, each round they do not 
behave as normal. Instead, roll a d6 on the table below to determine 
their action: 
 
Confusion 
d6 
Action 
1 
The subject attacks their friends if able. 
2 
The subject does nothing. 
3 
The subject does nothing. 
4 
The subject moves in a random direction. 
5 
The subject moves in a random direction. 
6 
The subject may act normally 
 
Requires Expert Craft (Alchemy) to create.

Codex of Golems 
A codex of golems is a very rare mago-alchemical formula book that 
allows a sufficiently intelligent creature to create magical golems. 
▶ Value: 3,000gp per tier. 
▶ Component Cost: 2,000gp per tier. 
▶ Craft Time: 2 days (16 hours) per tier. 
▶ Inventory Space: This item takes up one carrying capacity slot. 
▶ Required Skills/Talents to use: None. 
 
Tier 1 Effect: While using this codex, a creature may craft a 
clay golem. This takes two weeks, costs 11,000gp and requires 
Journeyman Craft (Magic Items) to create. At the end of the 
crafting time, the creator has one clay golem and a clay golem 
control rod, which causes the newly created golem to obey the 
commands of whoever wields the rod. 
 
The Codex requires Trained Craft (Magic Items) to 
create. 
 
Tier 2 Effect: While using this codex, a creature may craft a 
stone  golem. This takes three weeks, costs 14,000gp and 
requires Expert Craft (Magic Items) to create. At the end of 
the crafting time, the creator has one stone golem and a stone 
golem control rod, which causes the newly created golem to 
obey the commands of whoever wields the rod. 
 
The Codex requires Expert Craft (Magic Items) to create. 
 
Tier 3 Effect: While using this codex, a creature may craft an 
iron  golem. This takes four weeks, costs 16,000gp and 
requires Master Craft (Magic Items) to create. At the end of 
the crafting time, the creator has one iron golem and an iron 
golem control rod, which causes the newly created golem to 
obey the commands of whoever wields the rod. 
 
The Codex requires Grandmaster Craft (Magic Items) to 
create.

is for:

D6 
Golem Type 
1-4 
Clay (Tier 1) 
4-5 
Stone (Tier 2) 
6 
Iron (Tier 3)

gains a 4+ power save.

Requires Grandmaster Craft (Magic Items) to create.

Smoke Rod 
When snapped, this black rod emits billowing clouds of harmless 
black smoke. 
▶ Value: 250gp. 
▶ Component Cost: 110gp. 
▶ Craft Time: ½ a day (4 hours). 
▶ Inventory Space: This item takes up one carrying capacity slot. 
▶ Required Skills/Talents to use: None. 
 
Effect: The user breaks the rod in two and a cloud of smoke 
billows out from remains in a 15’ radius. The rod billows out 
continuously for 1 turn (10 minutes), maintaining this 15’ radius 
throughout. Ranged attack tests made against creatures in the 
smoke count as hard (-3). All creatures in the smoke gain +3 
Stealth tests.

Requires Trained Craft (Alchemy) to create. 
 
Spell Scroll (X level) 
Magic can be written on parchment and stored carefully until a time 
in which it is needed. 
▶ Value: 150gp (tier 1); 300gp (tier 2); 1,500gp (tier 3); 5,000gp (tier 
4); 10,000gp (tier 5). 
▶ Component Cost: A spell scroll costs 100gp x level of the spell + 
the cost of any spell components required to normally cast the spell. 
▶ Inventory Space: This item takes up one carrying capacity slot. 
▶ Craft Time: 1 day (8 hours) per tier. 
▶ Required Skills/Talents to use: None. 
Spell scrolls are the only magic items to go up to tier 5, with each 
tier representing a level of spell. To craft a spell scroll, a character 
must already know the spell they intend to copy onto a scroll. 
 
Tier 1 Effect: The creature may read the scroll to cast the 1st 
level spell, after which, the scroll burns itself up. This casting 
does not require any material components despite what the 
spell normally requires. 
 
Requires Trained Craft (Spells) to create. 
 
Tier 2 Effect: The creature may read the scroll to cast the 2nd 
level spell, after which, the scroll burns itself up. This casting 
does not require any material components despite what the 
spell normally requires. 
 
Requires Journeyman Craft (Spells) to create. 
 
Tier 3 Effect: The creature may read the scroll to cast the 3rd 
level spell, after which, the scroll burns itself up. This casting 
does not require any material components despite what the 
spell normally requires. 
 
Requires Expert Craft (Spells) to create. 
 
Tier 4 Effect: The creature may read the scroll to cast the 4th 
level spell, after which, the scroll burns itself up. 
 
Requires Master Craft (Spells) to create. This casting 
does not require any material components despite what 
the spell normally requires. 
 
Tier 5 Effect: The creature may read the scroll to cast the 5th 
level spell, after which, the scroll burns itself up. This casting 
does not require any material components despite what the 
spell normally requires. 
 
Requires Grandmaster Craft (Spells) to create. 
 
When this item is first generated, roll on the appropriate spell level 
table in Appendix 1 to see what spell is on the scroll.

Soul Jar 
This innocuous plain jar can store the soul of a creature, thus 
avoiding the certain fate of death. 
▶ Value: 16,000gp. 
▶ Component Cost: 10,000gp. 
▶ Craft Time: 2 weeks (112 hours). 
▶ Inventory Space: This item takes up one carrying capacity slot. 
▶ Required Skills/Talents to use: None. 
 
Effect: This seemingly plain jar can store a soul. If the creature 
carrying the jar dies, then their soul becomes stored in the jar. 
While in this jar the soul can do nothing apart from speak 
telepathically to any creature within 30’. They can also attempt 
to leave the jar, by entering an empty body within 30’ (such as 
recently deceased body). If the body is inhabited by somebody 
else, then the caster must succeed an opposed PER vs PER 
test to successfully enter the body. If they are successful, they 
enter the body, but the previous soul is not removed. Each day, 
the opposed test is repeated with a success indicating that that 
the creature has control of the body, and a failure indicating 
that the previous occupant has control. 
 
Requires Master Craft (Magic Items) to create.

Wand of Spells 
Wands can be used to increase the efficacy of a spellcaster with a 
particular spell. They are useful tools of wizards across the cosmos 
▶ Value: 3,000gp per tier. 
▶ Component Cost: 2,000gp per tier. 
▶ Craft Time: 3 days (24 hours) per tier. 
▶ Inventory Space: This item takes up one carrying capacity slot. 
▶ Required Skills/Talents to use: Spellcasting (Trained).

gains a +3 to the spellcasting test. In addition, the caster 
ignores the ‘forgotten’ entry on a spellcasting table and the spell 
costs 1 fewer FP to cast (but cannot be reduced below 0). 
 
A tier 1 wand of spells requires Trained Craft (Magic 
Items) to create. 
 
A tier 2 wand of spells requires Journeyman Craft (Magic 
Items) to create. 
 
A tier 3 wand of spells requires Expert Craft (Magic 
Items) to create.

to see what level of spell is contained in the wand. Then roll on the 
appropriate random spell table in Appendix 1. A wand of spells that 
is crafted by a character contains any spell the character knows, up 
to 3rd level.

159 
 
Bestiary
 
Reading a Bestiary Entry 
Each entry contains a number of abbreviations that are defined 
below: 
▶NA: Number Appearing. The number of this monster when rolled 
on a table. 
▶AL: Alignment. Describes a creature’s alignment. 
▶Lore: Describes the most common Knowledge specialism that can 
be tested to recall information about the creature. 
▶HP: Hit Points. This represents the general vitality and strength of 
a monster. 
▶AS: Armour Save. It tells you what the creature’s armour save is. 
If there is a second save listed, such as 5+/6+ then the second save 
is a power save. 
▶ Composition: Provides additional detail on how this creature is 
encountered. 
▶ Morale: Lists the morale value of the creature, representing how 
easily they flee from combat. 
▶Move: How far the creature can move in the move step. 
▶ Size: There are six different categories of size: tiny, small, 
medium, large, huge and gargantuan. Tiny creatures take up a 
negligible amount of space and up to four Tiny creatures can fit in 
a 5’x5’ area. Small creatures are about the size of a halfling and take 
up 5’x5’ space. Medium creatures are about the size of a human and 
take up 5’x5’ space. Large creatures are about the size of an ogre 
and take up 10’x10’ space. Huge creatures are about the size of a 
boreal worm and take up 15’x15’ space. Gargantuan creatures are 
about the size of a dragon and take up to or more than a 20’x20’ 
space. 
▶ STR; AGI; END; INT; PER: The core attributes of the creature, 
along with its proficiency level and target number. 
▶ Skills: The skills a creature has, along with its proficiency level 
and target number. 
▶ Resistance: If a creature has resistance to a damage type then any 
damage it takes from an attack is halved, to a minimum of one.  
▶ Vulnerability: If a creature has vulnerability to a damage type 
then any damage it takes from an attack is doubled.  
▶ Immune: If the creature is hit with this damage type, then the 
creature takes no damage. 
▶ Actions: Lists the actions that a creature may take, such as attacks 
or casting spells. Some actions will have a recharge. Once they have 
been used once, then a d6 is rolled at the start of each of the 
creature’s actions. On the listed value, the action is ready to use 
again. For attacks, see the weapon traits in the equipment section as 
needed. 
▶ Abilities: Lists other abilities that a creature may have. 
▶XP: Experience points. The number of experience points that 
killing or defeating this 
creature gives to the 
party.  
 
 
 
Albatross – Level ½, Small Animal (60xp) 
Albatrosses are otherwise mundane seabirds, yet there is something 
dangerously prophetic about their appearance. 
NA: Lair: 1d4 x 10 Warband: 1d6 Encounter: 1 
AL: TN; Lore: Nature 
HP: 1  AS: 6+ (none)  Morale: 17  Move: 10’ (30’ fly) 
 
STR: U (15); AGI: U (15); END: U (15); INT: U (15); PER: U (15) 
Skills: None 
Actions:  
▶ Peck Attack: Melee (15); 1 piercing. 
Abilities:  
▶ Conduit for Prophecy: If an albatross is encountered by creatures 
at sea it becomes a guardian for them, providing a ship’s crew with 
a +1 bonus to all tests while the albatross follows the ship. If an 
albatross following a ship is killed, then a terrible curse is placed 
upon the ship. Roll 1d6 on the table below to determine the curse: 
 
Albatross Curses 
d6 
Action 
1 
The creature which killed the albatross is struck dead 
immediately. 
2 
The creature which killed the albatross is cursed. They 
suffer a -6 to all tests while cursed. 
3 
The crew of the entire ship is cursed. They suffer a -3 
to all tests. 
4 
The crew of the entire ship is cursed. They may only 
step foot on land during a full moon. 
5 
The crew of the entire ship is cursed. If they step foot 
on land, they immediately die. 
6 
The albatross was actually a demon in disguise, the 
creature which killed it instantly gains 1 level.

coleop-terror’s eyes must save vs PER or become confused as 
described below: 
 
A creature that is confused may save vs PER at the end of each 
round to end the confusion. While confused, each round they do not 
behave as normal. Instead, roll a d6 on the table below to determine 
their action: 
 
Confusion 
d6 
Action 
1 
The subject attacks their friends if able. 
2 
The subject does nothing. 
3 
The subject does nothing. 
4 
The subject moves in a random direction. 
5 
The subject moves in a random direction. 
6 
The subject may act normally 
 
Conodith – Level 18, Large Monster (78,000xp) 
A conodith a psychic amphibian creature that lives in the deep 
underworld. They are capable of psionically dominating their foes, 
building vast cities of enslaved humanoids. 
NA: Lair: 1d6 Warband: 1 Encounter: 1 
AL: PE; Lore: Dungeoneering 
HP: 25  AS: 4+ (natural armour)  Morale: 7  Move: 30’ (40’ swim) 
STR: E (7); AGI: J (8); END: J (8); INT: M (6); PER: J (8) 
Skills: Melee: J (8)  
Resistances: Bludgeoning, piercing, and slashing from non-magical 
weapons. 
Actions:  
▶ 4 x Tentacle Whip: Melee (8); 7 slashing, rend (1). 
▶ Psionic Abilities: [Recharge 4+]: The conodith uses one of its 
potent psionic abilities, which are detailed below: 
 Mind Crush: One target within 30’ must save vs PER suffer 8 
arcane damage that ignores armour saves. A successful save 
halves the damage. 
 Stun Cone: All targets in a 30’ cone must save vs PER or gain 
2d4 stunned conditions each. 
 Dominate:  One target within 15’ must save vs PER or become 
psionically charmed by the conodith. While in this situation, 
they obey the conodith’s every command. They may retake this 
save vs PER based on their Intellect proficiency level: 
▶ Untrained: Once per week. 
▶ Trained and Journeyman: Once per day. 
▶ Expert: Once per hour. 
▶ Master: Once per turn (10 minutes). 
▶ Grandmaster: Once per round. 
Dramatic Actions: 2/round 
▶ Tentacle Whip: Melee (8); 7 slashing, rend (1). 
Abilities:  
▶ Creature of Legend: Automatically passes a save it was called 
upon to make. It can do this three times per day.

and dies in 1d4 rounds when the nautilid sucks away the last of their 
brain.

Occulator – Level 24, Large Monster (330,000xp) 
Occulators are nightmare aberrations – floating spherical creatures 
that sport a single large eye, supported by masses of smaller eyes, all 
of which can shoot deleterious beams. 
NA: Lair: 1 Warband: 1 Encounter: 1 
AL: PE; Lore: Dungeoneering 
HP: 31 AS: 3+/5+ (natural armour)  Morale: 5  Move: 20’ (20’ fly) 
STR: E (6); AGI: J (7); END: J (7); INT: M (5); PER: E (6) 
Skills: Melee: J (7)  
Resistances: All damage from non-magical sources. 
Actions:  
▶ 2 x Bite Attack: Melee (7); 8 piercing, rend (2). 
▶ 3 x Eye Beam Attack: The occulator shoots an eye ray. The ray 
used is determined randomly by rolling on the table below: 
 1. Charm: The target must hard (-3) save vs PER or become 
under the control of the occulator for 2d4 rounds. 
 2. Sleep: The target must hard (-3) save vs PER or fall into an 
unnatural sleep that lasts for 1d6 turns. 
 3. Petrification: The target must hard (-3) save vs END or take 
a slowed condition. If they gain anymore slowed conditions, 
then they turn to stone. 
 4. Telekinesis: The target must hard (-3) save vs AGI or be 
lifted off the ground. The occulator can move them up to 20’ 
per round while still using its other eye beams. 
 5. Disintegrate: The target must hard (-3) save vs AGI or take 
12 arcane damage, rend (2). If this reduces a creature to 0HP 
then they, and all of their equipment, are reduced to ash. 
 6. Death Ray: The target must make a hard (-3) save vs END 
or die. 
Dramatic Actions: 3/round 
▶ Bite Attack: Melee (7); 8 piercing, rend (2). 
▶ Eye Beam Attack: See above for details on the eye beam attack. 
▶ Shift: the occulator moves up to 30’, without provoking an attack 
of opportunity. 
Abilities:  
▶ Creature of Legend: Automatically passes a save it was called 
upon to make. It can do this three times per day. 
▶ Central Eye: The central eye of an occulator projects a 25’ cone 
of anti-magic. All magical items in the cone cease to function and all 
spells fail to cast.

Vampires are blood drinking horrors that have the curious ability to 
control and dominate those that serve them.

AL: PE; Lore: Religion 
HP: 18  AS: 6+/5+ (none)  Morale: 7  Move: 30’

points, provided it has not taken holy damage in the last round. It 
may even do this if it is dead.

Similarly, if a tentacle sustains 3 slashing damage in a round it will 
break off.

▶ Pounce: [Recharge 5+]: A worg leaps onto its foe. One creature 
adjacent to the worg must save vs AGI or be knocked prone, with 
the worg on top of them, taking 4 bludgeoning damage. While prone 
in this way, the target suffers a -2 to all armour saves taken against 
damage from the worg. 
Abilities:  
▶ Pack hunter: A worg gains a bonus to melee attack tests equal 
to every other ally within 5’ of the target. 
 
Wraith – Level 7, Medium Undead (1,980xp) 
When necromantic energies suffuse an area, negative memories and 
feelings can be reanimated into a wraith – an incorporeal undead 
horror. 
NA: Lair: 1d6 Warband: 1d4 Encounter: 1 
AL: PE; Lore: Religion 
HP: 8  AS: 6+ (none)  Morale: 1  Move: 30’ ( 30’ fly) 
STR: T (12); AGI: E (10); END: U (14); INT: T (12); PER: T (12) 
Skills: Melee: J (11) 
Immune: Magical charms and sleep and all non-magical damage. 
Vulnerabilities: Holy 
Actions:  
▶ Drain Vitality Attack: Melee (11); 3 cold damage that ignores 
armour saves. If this attack hits the target, they must save vs PER. 
A failure causes the target to lose 1d4 x 1000 XP, possibly lowering 
the character’s level. 
 
 
 
 
 
Wyvern – Level 8, Large Monster (8,220xp) 
Related to true dragons, wyverns are vicious creatures with 
prehensile, stinging tails. 
NA: Lair: 1d4+4 Warband: 1d4+4 Encounter: 1d4 
AL: PE; Lore: Arcana or Dungeoneering 
HP: 14  AS: 3+ (thick scales)  Morale: 7  Move: 30’ (60’ fly) 
STR: E (10); AGI: E (10); END: J (11); INT: U (14); PER: T (12) 
Skills: Melee: E (10) 
Actions:  
▶ 2 x Claws Attack; 1 x Bite: Melee (10); 5 slashing, rend (1); 
Melee (10); 4 piercing, rend (2). 
▶ Tail Sting: [Recharge 5+]: Melee (10); 3 piercing and target must 
save vs END or take an additional 3 poison damage that ignores all 
armour saves. 
 


Bestiary 
197 
 
Yeti – Level 4, Large Monster (820xp) 
These white-furred monsters are sometimes mistaken for apes, but 
their horned crowns and freezing breathes soon correct any errors. 
NA: Lair: 1d6 x 2 Warband: 1d6 Encounter: 1d3 
AL: WE; Lore: Arcana 
HP: 7  AS: 4+ (thick fur)  Morale: 10  Move: 30’ 
STR: E (11); AGI: T (13); END: J (12); INT: U (15); PER: U (15) 
Skills: Melee: E (11) 
Immune: Cold 
Actions:  
▶ Claw Attack: Melee (11); 5 slashing. 
▶ Cold Breath: [Recharge 5+]: A yeti exudes a 15’ cone of cold. All 
creatures within the cone suffer 2 cold damage, with a save vs AGI 
halving the damage. In addition, those failing the save also take a 
slowed condition for 1 turn. 
 
Zombie – Level 1, Medium Undead (75xp) 
The walking dead rise whenever evil or necromantic magic suffuses 
an area, dragging back a person’s anima from the afterlife. 
NA: Lair: 1d4 x 10 Warband: 1d6 x 5 Encounter: 1d6+6 
AL: PE; Lore: Religion 
HP: 2  AS: 6+ (none)  Morale: 1  Move: 30’ 
STR: T (13); AGI: U (15); END: T (13); INT: U (15); PER: U (15) 
Skills: Melee: T (13) 
Immune: Magical charms and sleep 
Vulnerabilities: Fire, Holy 
Actions:  
▶ Scratch and Bite Attack: Melee (13); 3 bludgeoning. 
▶ Reanimate: When killed, the zombie rolls a d6, on a 5+ it rises 
again with 1hp, provided they were not killed with fire or holy 
damage. 
 
 
Zombie, Hulk – Level 4, Large Undead (600xp) 
Sometimes, the walking dead are monstrously huge – reanimated 
ogres or trolls form into vast shambling hulks. 
NA: Lair: 1d4 x 2 Warband: 1d4+4  Encounter: 1d4 
AL: PE; Lore: Arcana 
HP: 7  AS: 6+ (none)  Morale: 1  Move: 30’ 
STR: E (13); AGI: U (15); END: J (12); INT: U (15); PER: U (15) 
Skills: Melee: T (13) 
Immune: Magical charms and sleep 
Vulnerabilities: Fire, Holy 
Actions:  
▶ Scratch and Bite Attack: Melee (13); 5 bludgeoning. 
▶ Reanimate: When killed, the zombie rolls a d6, on a 5+ it rises 
again with 1 hp, provided they were not killed with fire or holy 
damage. 
 
Zombie Wretch – Level 1, Medium Undead (37xp) 
The walking dead rise whenever evil or necromantic magic suffuses 
an area, dragging back a person’s anima from the afterlife. 
NA: Lair: 1d4 x 10 Warband: 1d6 x 5 Encounter: 1d6+6 
AL: PE; Lore: Religion 
HP: 1  AS: 6+ (none)  Morale: 1  Move: 30’ 
STR: T (13); AGI: U (15); END: T (13); INT: U (15); PER: U (15) 
Skills: Melee: T (13) 
Immune: Magical charms and sleep 
Vulnerabilities: Fire, Holy 
Actions:  
▶ Scratch and Bite Attack: Melee (13); 3 bludgeoning. 
▶ Reanimate: When killed, the zombie rolls a d6, on a 5+ it rises 
again with 1hp, provided they were not killed with fire or holy 
damage. 
Abilities:  
▶ Mook: This creature is a mook and follows the rules for mooks.
